ENTRY 0089 — 2002-02-03 — Food Rules

I tried to make rules for eating. No seconds.

No bread at dinner. No snacks after school.

I lasted two days and then ate peanut butter from the jar because I was starving. Afterward I felt disgusting. Mom found me in the kitchen and asked what I was doing.

I said nothing. She looked at the spoon, the jar, and my face.

She knew. She said, “We are not doing this.”

I got defensive and said she always tells me to eat healthy. She said healthy does not mean frightened of a sandwich.

We argued. I told her she does not know what it is like to be the biggest girl in the room.

She got very quiet. Then she said, “You don't know what I looked like at eleven.”

I had forgotten she used to be a girl. That sounds stupid.

Mothers should come with photographs attached.
ENTRY 0090 — 2002-02-14 — Valentine's Day

There were paper hearts everywhere at school. I got three little valentines from boys because everybody had to give everybody something. One was from Michael Kramer and I read it more carefully even though it said the exact same printed thing as the others.

This is embarrassing. Michael is not especially interesting. He still chews pencils.

But when he smiled at me in math yesterday I thought about it later. I do not know whether that counts as a crush.

Shannon says it does. Monique says a crush is just your brain choosing somebody to be stupid about for a while. I like that definition.

I am not telling Mom. She would be normal about it, which might be worse than teasing.
ENTRY 0091 — 2002-03-05 — Psychology Before I Know the Word

I found a book at the library about why people behave the way they do. It is not really a psychology textbook.

It is one of those books written for ordinary people. I like it anyway. There is a chapter about groups and how people do things in groups they might not do alone.

I thought about the kids laughing when Tyler made fun of my weight. Some of them were nice to me individually.

Then I thought about the fight and how everybody watching changed something too. I wonder how much of who we are belongs to us and how much is the room we are standing in. Dad says I am thinking too hard again.

I said that is not a real medical condition. He said he is pretty sure it runs in my family.
ENTRY 0092 — 2002-03-17 — Dad's Birthday Was Yesterday, Sort Of

Dad's birthday was actually yesterday because his birthday is March 18 and I got the dates mixed up in my head all week. Mom laughed at me for being the child who remembers historical dates but nearly celebrates her father's birthday on the wrong day. We gave him a new welding hood lens he wanted and a dinner he did not have to grill himself. I asked what he wanted to do when he was my age.

He said, “Get out.”

I asked out of what. He said poverty, mostly.

That answer stayed with me. He said he did not dream about welding. He got good at it because good welders got paid.

Then he said being good at something can become its own kind of love even if it wasn't the first dream. I wonder what my first dream is.

I am not sure I have one yet.
ENTRY 0093 — 2002-04-01 — Science Fair

My science fair project is about corrosion. Dad helped me set up pieces of metal in different conditions: salt water, plain water, dry air, and oil-coated. Mr.

Bennett made sure I could explain it without saying “my dad told me.”

I can. I like watching something change slowly enough that you have to measure it.

Salt water is brutal. Dad says boats are worse because salt gets into everything. I told him we do not own a boat.

He said that is why he sleeps at night. I won second place.

A boy won first with a volcano that actually erupted all over the table. I am trying not to be bitter about this obvious corruption of scientific standards. Shannon says second place is good.

It is. I wanted first.

Both things can be true. There it is again.
ENTRY 0094 — 2002-04-20 — Jason's Baseball Future

A college coach came to one of Jason's games. Dad pretended not to care.

He cared so much he cleaned the car before we left. Jason was tense all morning and snapped at everybody.

He played well, but not perfectly. On the drive home nobody knew what the coach thought. Jason stared out the window.

I realized something then: being the kid everybody watches is not only good. I have spent years jealous of the cheering.

I never thought about what it feels like when everybody's hopes climb into the batter's box with you. At home I told Jason I thought he played good. He said, “Well.”

I said, “See?

This is why nobody loves you.”

He laughed. I think that helped more than telling him everything would work out.
ENTRY 0095 — 2002-05-24 — Fourth Grade Ends

Fourth grade is over. No expulsions. No repeating.

I should get a certificate just for administrative stability. Mr. Bennett wrote in my report that I am “analytical, highly verbal, and sometimes reluctant to take social risks.”

Mom laughed at “sometimes.”

I asked what social risks means.

She said things like raising my hand, joining groups, trying things where other people can see me fail. That is annoyingly accurate.

I am going to fifth grade next year. I will be eleven turning twelve while some kids are ten. I am noticing the age difference more now because bodies are changing at different speeds.

Mine seems determined to arrive everywhere early. I wish the rest of me could catch up.
ENTRY 0096 — 2002-06-12 — Grandma June's Birthday

Grandma June is sixty-five today. She gave me a birthday present on her own birthday.

This is extremely Grandma June. It was a little metal toolbox Grandpa Walter used years ago. She said he has a bigger one now and this one was “just sitting.”

Grandpa Walter said, “It was not just sitting.

I knew where it was.”

Grandma ignored him. The box is scratched and heavy.

It smells faintly like old oil. Dad helped me move my sockets and multimeter into it. He looked at the box for a long time and said he remembered Grandpa taking it to work when Dad was a kid.

Now it is mine. I like old things that have already belonged to people.

New things feel like objects. Old things sometimes feel like witnesses.
ENTRY 0097 — 2002-07-04 — Fireworks Again

I sat on the Barracuda hood again this year. Dad looked at me and said, “I'm not making the joke.”

I knew exactly which joke. I laughed. That might be proof apologies actually work.

The Heart tape played on the drive to the fireworks. I know more of the songs now.

I like the ones that sound like somebody is trying to break out of something. I do not know what I am trying to break out of. Maybe other people's eyes.

Maybe my own. At the field, some boys from school ran past and one shouted my name.

I stiffened automatically. He just wanted to ask if we had extra sparklers. My body prepares for insult before my brain knows there is one.

I wonder how long that takes to unlearn.
ENTRY 0098 — 2002-07-30 — Chinese Food

Mom and I got Chinese takeout after errands because Dad and the boys were gone. I love Chinese food. I also love Mexican food, which Mom says proves I mostly like food that comes with too many choices.

If we have money for quick food, I would pick Chinese or Mexican before pizza most days. For chains I like Chick-fil-A better than most burger places, although Burger King fries when they are actually hot are good.

Panera feels like where adults go to pretend soup is a complete event. Chipotle is dangerous because I always think I can fit more in a burrito than physics allows. Mom laughed at me because I spent five minutes deciding between sesame chicken and lo mein.

She said, “For somebody who thinks about God and free will, you are defeated by a laminated menu.”

I told her decisions are harder when all outcomes contain noodles. This is the kind of evening I never think is important while it is happening.

Maybe that is why I should write it down.
ENTRY 0099 — 2002-08-15 — Fifth Grade Clothes

School shopping again. My body is definitely ahead of where I want it to be.

Mom bought me bras in a size I refuse to write down because seeing the number makes it feel official. She also made me try shirts that fit instead of automatically buying everything huge.

One dark red shirt actually looked good. I stared at myself for a long time. My waist is there now even though I am still heavy.

My chest is obvious. My face looks less round than last year.

I could see, for one second, why Mom says I am pretty. Then I immediately started finding things wrong. It is like my brain has a job and the job is to make sure I never get too comfortable in a mirror.

I bought the red shirt anyway. I am going to wear it the first week of school.

Maybe.
ENTRY 0100 — 2002-09-05 — Fifth Grade, Older, Still Me

Fifth grade has been going for two weeks. I am one of the oldest kids in class. I am also one of the most developed girls, which means people notice things I wish they would not notice.

A boy looked at my chest today and then looked away so fast it was almost funny. Almost. I wore the red shirt.

I spent the first hour pulling it down, then got tired of myself and stopped. Mr.

Bennett is not my teacher anymore. Mrs. Park is.

She gave us an assignment to write three things we are good at and three things we want to improve. I wrote:

Good at: reading, fixing small things, noticing when people are left out.

Improve: math when I get impatient, speaking in front of people, controlling my temper. I did not write losing weight. I almost did.

That feels important. The fight was two and a half years ago now.

I am still the girl who did it, but I am not only that girl. I am the girl with Grandpa Walter's old toolbox. The girl who knows how to test a battery.

The girl who reads books that are too hard until they aren't. The girl who gets jealous and scared and angry and sometimes still says the wrong thing.

I think maybe a life is just refusing to let one sentence be the whole story.

ENTRY 0101 — 2002-10-04 — The Red Shirt Again

I wore the red shirt again today. That should not deserve an entry, but apparently it does because I stood in front of the mirror for ten minutes deciding whether I could handle being seen in it.

At school, nothing happened. Nobody laughed. Nobody announced that I had breasts.

Nobody threw tomatoes. Shannon said she liked the color and then asked if I had finished the science worksheet.

My brain had prepared for a public execution and received fractions instead. At lunch a boy named Aaron asked if he could borrow a pencil. His eyes went where boys' eyes keep going lately, then he looked embarrassed.

I felt angry and weirdly powerful and then angry at myself for feeling powerful. I came home and changed into Dad's old gray sweatshirt.

Mom asked why. I said I was cold. She looked at the thermostat and did not argue.

Sometimes Mom lets me keep a lie if she understands what it is protecting.
ENTRY 0102 — 2002-10-20 — Twelve Is Coming

I will be twelve in a little over two weeks. Because I repeated third grade, twelve feels older than it should. Some girls in my class are still ten. I hear adults say, “At this age a year doesn't matter,” which is obviously something adults say because a year does not make them the oldest girl in fifth grade.

Kelly is thirteen and acts as if she has crossed an ocean I have not. She wears lip gloss now and says things like “when you get to middle school” in a voice that makes me want to throw something.

Then she lets me borrow her CDs and tells me which boys are idiots, so I forgive her. Dad says age matters less the older you get because the percentage gets smaller. He actually did the math on a napkin.

At twelve, one year is more than eight percent of your whole life. At fifty, it is two percent.

I told him this does not make me feel better. He said, “Wasn't trying to. Just giving you the math.”

That is Dad.
ENTRY 0103 — 2002-11-06 — Twelve

I am twelve. Mom made tacos because I asked for them instead of a “birthday dinner.” She fried the shells herself and let me put too much cheese on everything. Dad gave me a better multimeter.

An actual one, not the little cheap meter from his junk drawer. He wrote TAMMY on the back in silver marker and said, “Now quit stealing mine.”

I hugged him so hard he laughed into my hair.

Jason gave me twenty dollars, which means he either likes me more than I thought or forgot until this afternoon. Mike gave me a St. Louis Blues cap and said I can finally stop wearing Dad's giant one.

Shannon called after dinner and sang Happy Birthday badly on purpose. I looked at the candles and realized this is the third birthday since the fight.

That used to be how I counted time. Before the fight. After the fight.

Tonight I forgot to think about it until now. Maybe that is what healing is sometimes.

Not forgiveness or a lesson. Just forgetting to measure everything from the wound.
ENTRY 0104 — 2002-11-24 — Grandma June's Foil Drawer

We had an early Thanksgiving at Grandma June's because Jason has a baseball thing next weekend. Grandma has a drawer full of folded aluminum foil.

Not rolls. Pieces.

She washes foil, dries it, folds it, and saves it unless it has a hole. Mom says she grew up with people who never believed there would definitely be more of anything later. I asked Grandma if she ever buys foil.

She said, “Of course I buy foil. That's how I get more to save.”

Dad nearly choked on his tea.

I helped her wrap leftovers. She gave me a container that used to hold margarine and warned me not to throw it away. I said, “What if I die before I return it?”

She said, “Then I'll speak to your mother.”

I love her.

On the drive home I thought about how our family treats objects. We fix cars.

We save jars. Dad keeps bolts in coffee cans. Mom mends towels until they are more patch than towel.

Maybe being poor is partly learning that objects have second lives.
ENTRY 0105 — 2002-12-15 — Shannon Is Twelve

Shannon turned twelve today. Her mother let her have four girls sleep over. I almost did not go because two of the girls are the kind who can make silence feel like an insult, but Shannon called me and said, “If you don't come, I have to survive them alone.”

So I went.

We ate pizza, painted our nails terribly, and watched movies. At about midnight the conversation became boys. One girl said she had kissed somebody behind the gym.

I did not believe her, but everybody acted impressed. Then someone asked me who I liked.

I said nobody. That was mostly true. There are boys I think are cute.

There are boys I would like to notice me until they actually look at me. Then I want to turn invisible.

Shannon saved me by throwing a pillow at somebody and starting a war. Later, when the others were asleep, she whispered, “You know you don't have to tell people stuff just because they ask.”

I said I knew. I did know.

It was still nice hearing it.
ENTRY 0106 — 2003-01-03 — The Heater

The furnace quit last night. Of course it did it when the temperature dropped into the teens.

Dad came home tired, ate half a sandwich standing up, and crawled under the trailer with a flashlight. I held tools near the access panel and tried not to complain about the cold. The problem was not dramatic.

A bad connection and a filthy sensor. Dad cleaned it, remade the connection, and the furnace came back on.

I said, “That's it?”

He said, “Most broken things are less interesting than the story people tell about them.”

I asked if that is why mechanics charge so much. He laughed. When the heat started blowing again, Mom stood over the vent in her socks like she had personally discovered fire.

We could not afford a big repair right now. I know because I heard Mom and Dad talking quietly in the kitchen before Christmas.

I am starting to understand why Dad teaches me everything. It is not just because he likes having me around. Knowing how things work is a kind of money.
ENTRY 0107 — 2003-01-28 — A Bra Strap

A boy snapped my bra strap today. I hate even writing that because it sounds small. It hurt. Not much.

Mostly it surprised me. I turned around and shoved his hand away and said, “Do that again and lose it.”

Mrs.

Park heard the end, not the beginning. Of course. We both got pulled aside.

When I explained, she looked at him and said touching somebody's clothing without permission is not funny. He muttered that he was just joking.

I said, “I wasn't.”

Mrs. Park looked at me very carefully. I knew what she was remembering from my record even if she never said it.

I kept my hands at my sides. That mattered to me more than the whole rest of the thing.

I was furious and I did not hit him. Dad said that is exactly the difference he meant three years ago. I wanted him to be proud of me.

He was.
ENTRY 0108 — 2003-02-14 — Valentines

School valentines are cruel because teachers make everybody give one to everybody, so you can tell which ones mean nothing. Shannon gave me one with a stupid cartoon frog and wrote, “You are weird. Stay weird.”

That was my favorite.

Aaron gave everybody the same little card, but mine had my name written more carefully. I noticed.

I wish I had not noticed. At church Wednesday we had talked about guarding your heart. Adults use phrases like that as if hearts are purses somebody might steal if you leave them unattended.

I do not know how you guard a heart without shutting it in a closet. Mom and Dad went out for dinner tonight, which they almost never do.

Jason complained because Mom made us frozen pizza. I told him Valentine's Day is for romance and he said Mom and Dad are too old for romance. Dad came back through the door behind him and said, “Son, that belief is how family lines end.”

I laughed so hard I nearly dropped my glass.
ENTRY 0109 — 2003-03-09 — The Library Card Is Soft Now

My library card is worn soft at the edges. The plastic layer is peeling.

Mrs. Haney says she can replace it, but I do not want a new one yet.

I like that this one looks used. I checked out a book about the brain today because I wanted to know why people remember embarrassing things more clearly than ordinary things. The book says emotion helps memories get stored.

That feels unfair. If I am going to remember Tyler calling me fat in third grade forever, I should also remember every time somebody was kind.

So I tried tonight. Alvarez telling me I was a strong reader. Dad writing my name on the multimeter.

Mom pretending not to know why I changed shirts. Shannon sitting beside me when I came to the new school.

Grandma June giving me Grandpa's toolbox. There are more than I thought. Maybe memory needs help choosing what gets repeated.
ENTRY 0110 — 2003-03-29 — Mike's First Real Job

Mike got a weekend job helping a man from church with air conditioners. He says he is “in HVAC,” which Dad says is a fancy way of saying Mike now carries heavy things for somebody who knows what he is doing. Mike came home with cuts on his hands and fifty dollars.

He put ten dollars on the kitchen table for Mom. She tried to give it back. He said, “I ate here all week, didn't I?”

She got that look where she is proud and upset at the same time.

I watched all of this from the couch. Money in our house is rarely just money.

It is apology, independence, fear, pride, love, control, proof that you are contributing. I wonder what my first job will be. I want to earn money.

I also do not want money to become the only reason I have permission to take up space. I am twelve, so maybe that is too dramatic.

But it feels true.
ENTRY 0111 — 2003-04-19 — April's Birthday

April is thirteen today. She got a denim skirt and a little silver necklace from Aunt Denise.

She kept checking herself in the mirror and then pretending she was not checking. I know that move because I do it too. She asked if I had started shaving my legs.

I said no. She said, “You need to.”

I looked down at my legs and suddenly saw hair that had apparently been there all morning without being a crisis.

Thanks, April. Later she apologized without exactly apologizing. She handed me the razor package her mom bought and said there were extras if I wanted one.

I told Mom that night. She said I can shave if I want to, but I do not owe smooth legs to civilization.

That sounded surprisingly radical coming from my mother. I shaved one shin to see what it felt like. Now I have one smooth leg and one normal leg.

This may be the stupidest experiment I have ever conducted.
ENTRY 0112 — 2003-05-08 — Health Class

We had the puberty talk at school. It was terrible. The boys went somewhere else and the girls stayed with the nurse, which made everybody immediately understand that something horrible was coming. She talked about periods, bras, body odor, hair, mood changes, and reproduction.

Some girls giggled until they were almost crying. I sat very still.

Most of the things she described are already happening to me. One girl asked if it is normal to develop early. The nurse said there is a wide range.

I wanted to raise my hand and ask, “How wide?”

I did not. She passed around pads in little wrappers.

I put mine in my backpack like contraband. At home Mom saw it and asked if I had questions. I said no.

Then five minutes later I asked her seventeen questions while she was chopping onions. She answered every one.

I am glad she does not make my body sound dirty. Church sometimes makes bodies sound like dangerous equipment. Mom mostly makes mine sound like mine.
ENTRY 0113 — 2003-05-26 — Memorial Day Garage Day

Dad worked on the Barracuda most of today. Not because anything was badly wrong. He said old cars need attention before they demand it.

We changed oil and checked hoses. He showed me how rubber can look fine from the top and crack when you bend it.

“Inspection means touching things,” he said. I told him that sounded like something Mr. Cline would object to.

Dad stared at me for one second and then laughed so hard he had to put the wrench down. I am getting old enough to make jokes that surprise him.

The Heart tape played while we worked. The deck changed tracks with that heavy clunk I love. Dad said one day nobody will know what that sound means.

I said I will. He looked at me and said, “Then it won't be nobody.”

That stuck with me.
ENTRY 0114 — 2003-06-06 — Fifth Grade Finished

Fifth grade is over. I am officially going to middle school.

I am excited and terrified. Mrs.

Park wrote that I am “exceptionally thoughtful” and should “continue developing confidence in public participation.”

This is teacher language for Tammy knows the answer and refuses to raise her hand. Shannon and I walked home partway together. We promised we will stay friends even if our schedules are different next year.

Promises like that make me nervous because people only make them when they know change is coming. I packed my school papers into a box tonight.

There was the three-things-I-am-good-at assignment from September. Reading. Fixing small things.

Noticing people left out. Still true.

I wrote a fourth underneath in pen:

Getting back up. That one sounds corny. I am keeping it anyway.
ENTRY 0115 — 2003-06-28 — Pool

Kelly invited me to the public pool. I almost said no. Swimsuits feel like volunteering evidence against yourself.

Mom bought me a one-piece with a high front because she knew I would refuse anything else. It still showed more of my chest than I wanted. At the pool I spent the first fifteen minutes with my arms crossed.

Then Shannon jumped in and splashed me on purpose. After that I forgot for a while.

That is the strange thing about my body. When I am using it, swimming or carrying something or helping Dad, it belongs to me. When I am standing still and somebody is looking, it becomes an object I am responsible for managing.

A group of older boys looked over at us. One whispered something.

Kelly flipped them off under the water where Aunt Ruth could not see. I laughed. Maybe I should learn that gesture.

Mom would die.
ENTRY 0116 — 2003-07-12 — Dad's Hands

Dad burned two fingers at work this week. Not badly, but the skin is red and blistered.

He says it happened through the glove because the piece was hotter than he thought. I asked if he gets scared welding. He said, “You don't want a welder who isn't scared of heat.”

I understood what he meant.

Fear is information. He let me help clean the garage tonight because gripping things hurts him.

I sorted sockets and swept metal filings. His work boots leave black dust on the floor even when he wipes them. The smell of welding is part of Dad to me.

Metal, smoke, sweat, soap afterward. I cannot imagine him without it.

I wrote that sentence and suddenly felt stupidly afraid. People do not stay the same forever. Dad is forty-one.

That seems old and not old at all.
ENTRY 0117 — 2003-07-31 — Before Middle School

I had a dream I showed up to middle school naked. Apparently my brain is a cliché. In the dream nobody screamed. Everybody just looked disappointed.

That is worse somehow. I told Shannon and she said she dreamed she could not find her locker and ended up living in the hallway until college.

We spent the afternoon practicing combinations on Jason's old padlock. Middle school has lockers, changing classes, gym uniforms, and older kids. The older-kid part worries me most.

I am already physically older-looking than many girls my age, and now I will be around seventh and eighth graders who may assume I am older than I am. Mom says if anybody makes me uncomfortable I can leave.

“Even if I'm rude?”

“Especially if you're rude.”

I wrote that down.
ENTRY 0118 — 2003-08-17 — It Started

My period started today. I knew what it was immediately and still thought I was dying for about three seconds. There was blood in my underwear and my whole body went cold.

Then I remembered health class. I yelled for Mom through the bathroom door.

She came in with pads and clean underwear and did not make it ceremonial, which I appreciate. She showed me what to do even though I technically knew. I cried afterward.

Not because it hurt much. I just did.

Mom hugged me and said hormones make everything louder sometimes. I asked if this means I can get pregnant now. She said biologically, yes, which is why growing up comes with more responsibility than people admit.

I am twelve. My body has apparently filed paperwork declaring itself older.

I hate that it can do things before I am ready to think about them.
ENTRY 0119 — 2003-08-25 — Sixth Grade

First day of middle school. I survived.

My locker opened on the first try, which felt like divine intervention. Shannon has lunch with me but only two classes.

The halls are loud and everybody walks like they know exactly where they are going, even when they clearly do not. Gym is going to be hell. We have to change clothes in a locker room.

I kept my shirt on as long as possible and changed underneath it. A girl named Brooke said, “Why are you being weird?”

I said, “Because I enjoy mystery.”

She rolled her eyes.

I am one of the biggest-chested girls in the grade and I know people notice. A seventh-grade boy asked if I was new and what grade I was in. When I said sixth he looked surprised.

I wanted to ask which part of me he thought was in seventh. I did not.

Progress.
ENTRY 0120 — 2003-09-07 — Sunday Clothes

I cried before church over a blouse. This is embarrassing even for me. Mom bought it because it was on sale and nice enough for Sunday.

It buttons in front. The buttons pull across my chest. Not enough to actually open, but enough that I can see the little gaps.

I said I would not wear it. Mom said we do not have money to buy another blouse just because I am self-conscious.

I said, “Then I'm not going.”

That was the wrong thing to say. We fought. I accused her of not understanding because she was never built like me.

She said I do not get to punish everybody because I am unhappy with my body. I wore a cardigan over it and went.

After church she apologized for saying “punish.”

I apologized for yelling. Then she said we can move the buttons a little. Sometimes problems really are one inch of thread.
ENTRY 0121 — 2003-09-26 — Monique

I met Monique Harris in science. Technically I have known who she is for weeks.

Today we actually talked. Mr. Feldman paired us for a lab.

I warned her I am bossy when measurements are involved. She said, “Good.

I'm bossy when people are wrong.”

I liked her immediately. We had to measure how fast water cools. She drew a little face on the cup and named it Harold.

When our reading was different from the class average, another boy said we must have messed up. Monique said, “Or reality failed to consult your expectations.”

I almost snorted water through my nose.

She raises her hand constantly and never seems embarrassed when she is wrong. I asked her how. She said, “If I'm wrong, now I know something I didn't know five minutes ago.

That's not humiliating. That's efficient.”

I have been thinking about that all evening.
ENTRY 0122 — 2003-10-11 — The Period Emergency

My period came at school unexpectedly. I had a pad in my bag because Mom told me to always keep one there. Of course I forgot to move the bag when we went to gym. I felt it and knew.

Panic. Shannon saw my face and asked what was wrong.

I whispered it. She took off her sweatshirt and tied it around my waist without making a big deal. Then she went to the nurse with me.

There was a small stain. Probably nobody else noticed.

It felt enormous. I cried in the bathroom anyway. Shannon said, “You know half the planet does this, right?”

I said, “Not half the planet in these pants.”

She laughed and that saved me.

I washed them in the sink at home. Mom showed me how cold water works better.

There are entire categories of knowledge women pass to each other quietly because nobody else is going to.
ENTRY 0123 — 2003-10-31 — Halloween

I did not wear a costume this year. I told myself I am too old. Really, most costumes for girls my age seem designed either to make us look like little children or to make us look older in ways that make me uncomfortable.

Shannon dressed as a witch because witches can apparently be any age. I went with her while she took her little cousin around.

A group of boys from school passed us and one said, “Nice pumpkins,” while looking directly at my chest. I knew exactly what he meant. My face went hot.

Shannon said, “At least she has some,” which was not a great comeback, but the boys shut up. I was angry at them, grateful to her, and annoyed that her defense was still about my breasts.

There is no winning sometimes. At home I stole three mini Snickers from Mike's bag. That part of Halloween remains excellent.
ENTRY 0124 — 2003-11-06 — Thirteen

Thirteen. Official teenager.

I expected to feel different this morning. I felt sleepy.

Mom made enchiladas because I asked. Dad brought home a tiny socket set in a red metal case. He said it is for the glove box one day when I have my own car.

“One day?” I asked. He said, “I am not lending you mine forever.”

This is interesting because he has never said I can drive the Barracuda.

I wrote down that he implied it. Evidence matters. Kelly gave me lip gloss that tastes like fake strawberries.

April gave me a bracelet and told me I need to stop wearing men's sweatshirts. I wore Dad's sweatshirt while opening it.

Thirteen sounds like something should begin. Maybe nothing begins exactly when the number changes. Maybe it has already been beginning.
ENTRY 0125 — 2003-11-27 — Thanksgiving Table Politics

Grandpa Harold and Grandpa Walter argued about unions during Thanksgiving. I did not understand all of it, but I understood enough to know neither one was going to change his mind. Dad kept eating potatoes.

Mom kicked Jason under the table when he tried to join in. Afterward I asked Dad which grandfather was right. He said, “Depends what question you're asking.”

I hate that answer because it is usually the intelligent one.

He explained that a union can protect people from bad employers and can also make stupid decisions like any group of people. Then he told me never to trust someone who explains working people using only one story.

I asked if that was political. He said, “Everything gets political when somebody wants your labor.”

I wrote it down because it sounded important. Grandma June packed pie in a Cool Whip container.

Some traditions are easier to understand.
ENTRY 0126 — 2003-12-15 — Shannon's Party, Again

Shannon is thirteen now. We had another sleepover, but this one felt different from last year.

More talk about boys. Less pillow fighting. Everyone checking phones even though almost none of us actually have our own.

Brooke claimed an eighth grader asked for her number. Monique said, “Your house number?

Did he want to speak to your father?”

I laughed so hard Brooke threw popcorn at her. Somebody asked if I had kissed anybody. I said no.

Nobody acted shocked. That should have made me feel better.

Instead I wondered if they expected me to say no. There is a terrible little committee in my head whose entire purpose is turning neutral facts into insults. I would like to fire them.
ENTRY 0127 — 2004-01-08 — Locker Room Rules

I have developed a complete system for changing in gym without being naked for more than four seconds. Shirt off while gym shirt is already halfway on. Bra stays on. Pants changed facing locker.

Do not bend over if boys are passing the open doorway outside. Ignore comments.

Monique says the fact that girls have to learn these maneuvers is ridiculous. She changes clothes like she is alone in her bedroom and does not care who sees her stomach. I envy that.

Today Brooke asked what size bra I wear. I said, “The size that fits.”

She laughed and asked again.

I said, “Why do you need the data?”

Monique nearly applauded. I am learning that questions do not become obligations just because somebody asks them.
ENTRY 0128 — 2004-01-26 — Dad's Welding Hood

Dad brought home an old welding hood that was being thrown away at work. The lens is scratched and the headband is cracked. He said I could take it apart if I wanted.

I did. There are fewer parts than I expected.

Dad explained how the lens filters the arc. He made me promise never to look at an arc without proper protection even “just for a second.”

He said your eyes can get burned like skin. That sentence is horrifying.

I asked if his eyes have ever been flashed. He said once when he was young and stupid and he spent the night feeling like there was sand under his eyelids.

I said, “You were stupid?”

He said, “Frequently. That's why I have stories.”

I like discovering Dad had a life before he was Dad.
ENTRY 0129 — 2004-02-14 — Nobody

No valentine from a secret admirer. I know because I checked my locker twice like an idiot.

There was a candy gram from Shannon that said, “For my favorite emotionally complicated mechanic.”

I gave her one that said, “For my favorite liar who claims she does not like Eric Mason.”

She hit me with a notebook. I sometimes think I want a boyfriend.

Then I imagine an actual boy knowing things about me and I want to move to Canada. What I think I want is to be chosen. That is not the same thing, probably.

Church tonight was about love being patient and kind. I wonder why adults use that passage at weddings when most thirteen-year-olds need it more.
ENTRY 0130 — 2004-03-03 — Renee's Letter

Renee wrote back. I had almost stopped expecting it. Her handwriting is different now.

She said they moved again. She said school is okay. She said she remembers the fight but does not think about it much anymore.

Then she wrote, “I was mad at you for a while because everybody talked about you and nobody asked me what happened.”

I read that sentence five times. I had never thought of it like that.

I thought I was the one who got punished. She was the person being hurt and then the whole story became Tammy Beat Up Tasha. I wrote back and told her I was sorry.

Not sorry I tried to help. Sorry I made myself the biggest thing in a moment that started with her.

Dad's sentence again. Two things can be true in the same minute.
ENTRY 0131 — 2004-03-22 — Weight at the Doctor

Doctor appointment. I hate the scale.

The nurse says the number out loud as if she is announcing a bus schedule. I stared at the wall. The doctor said I am healthy but carrying more weight than ideal and that puberty makes bodies change quickly.

I heard “fat.”

Mom heard the actual sentence. In the car she asked what I heard.

I said, “That I'm fat.”

She said, “That is not what she said.”

I snapped at her. Then I cried. Again.

I am tired of crying about a body that is doing exactly what bodies do. Mom said we can walk together after dinner if I want, not to punish me but because both of us could use movement.

I said maybe. I do not want my life to become a diet.
ENTRY 0132 — 2004-04-04 — Walking

Mom and I have been walking after dinner. Not every night. Three or four times a week. We go around the park and sometimes farther down the road if there is still light.

She does not talk about calories. I appreciate this more than I can explain.

Tonight she told me stories about when she and Dad were dating. Dad had one good shirt and a car that overheated. Apparently he once took her to dinner and then spent forty minutes in the parking lot waiting for the engine to cool.

Mom said she knew she loved him because she was not bored. That might be my favorite definition so far.
ENTRY 0133 — 2004-04-19 — April Is Fourteen

April turned fourteen and had a party with boys invited. This is apparently a major developmental stage. I wore jeans and a black top Kelly helped me choose.

It fit my body instead of hiding it. I felt exposed for the first hour.

Then a boy named Nathan talked to me about the Blues for twenty minutes without looking down my shirt once. I noticed that too. Is that sad?

That basic eye contact feels impressive? He did not ask for my number or anything dramatic.

We just talked. On the drive home I replayed every sentence anyway. Fourteen-year-old April would laugh at me.

I am thirteen. I am allowed to be ridiculous.
ENTRY 0134 — 2004-05-02 — Church Questions

Mr. Cline taught about modesty tonight.

I knew I was going to hate it before he got through the first verse. He said boys and girls both have responsibilities not to tempt each other.

I raised my hand. I asked if a girl is responsible for what somebody thinks when she is wearing normal clothes that fit. The room got very quiet.

Cline said no, not entirely, and that modesty is about intention and judgment, not blaming someone for having a body. I respected him for answering.

Then one of the older women later told Mom I am “very mature physically” and should be especially careful. There it was. My body becomes evidence before I do anything.

Mom told me in the car that I am responsible for my choices, not for being shaped like myself. I needed that sentence.
ENTRY 0135 — 2004-05-24 — The Science Fair

I won second place at the science fair. Second. I am annoyed.

This is unattractive but true. My project was about resistance in electrical circuits. Dad helped me build the test board, but I did the measurements and graphs.

First place went to a volcano model with lights. A volcano.

With lights. Monique said I am becoming a bitter old scientist at thirteen. Dad said judging is subjective and if I want perfectly fair outcomes I should work with machines.

I said machines break unfairly too. He said, “Now you're learning.”

I put the ribbon over my desk anyway.
ENTRY 0136 — 2004-06-04 — Sixth Grade Finished

Finished sixth grade. No disasters.

No fights. No expulsions. One humiliating period stain, several stupid boys, many books, one second-place science fair robbery.

I did not take it back.
ENTRY 0137 — 2004-06-26 — First Time Driving

Dad let me steer the Barracuda in an empty parking lot. I am writing that again because it was glorious. I did not drive on a road. I barely drove at all.

Dad worked the pedals from the passenger side when he could and kept one hand ready near the wheel. The car felt enormous.

The hood goes on forever. I turned too sharply the first time and Dad said, “Easy. Don't fight it.”

The second lap was smoother.

The Heart tape was in the deck. I could feel the engine through the seat.

I have never loved a machine before. Maybe I have now.
ENTRY 0138 — 2004-07-09 — Jason Is Leaving More

Jason spends more time away now. Baseball, friends, work, girls. He is nineteen and acts like the trailer is a motel where the staff is disappointing.

Mom and he fought tonight because he came home after midnight without calling. Dad barely said anything until Jason raised his voice at Mom.

Then Dad said, very quietly, “Try that sentence again.”

Jason did. Much nicer. I watched from the hallway.

It is strange seeing my brother become an adult badly in real time. I hope I do it more gracefully.

Probably not.
ENTRY 0139 — 2004-07-28 — The Book About Depression

I checked out a book about depression. Not because I think I have it.

Because I heard a woman at church say her sister “just needs to count her blessings,” and something about that made me mad. The book says depression can change sleep, appetite, concentration, energy, even how pain feels.

That sounds like illness, not ingratitude. I told Mom. She said people sometimes turn things into moral failures when they do not understand them.

I asked if church does that. She said, “Church is people, Tammy.”

That answer covers a lot.
ENTRY 0140 — 2004-08-15 — Seventh Grade Clothes

Back-to-school shopping. My chest has grown again. I wanted to throw every bra in the store into traffic.

April says I should enjoy it because girls pay money for what I have. I said I would sell her half.

She said, “Deal.”

ENTRY 0141 — 2004-08-24 — Mike Is Seventeen

Mike turned seventeen. He got a used set of gauges for his HVAC work and was more excited than he would admit.

ENTRY 0142 — 2004-09-02 — Mom Is Forty

Mom is forty. She says this is impossible because she was twenty-seven last Tuesday. We made breakfast before church. Dad burned the first pancakes and blamed the pan.

Mom got flowers from him and a new purse from us. I asked if forty feels old.

She said, “Some mornings. Then I see your grandmother climbing a ladder to clean gutters and reconsider.”

I asked if she wishes she had done anything differently. She thought for a long time.

Then she said, “I wish I had believed earlier that choices I made willingly were still choices. I spent too much time acting trapped by things I had chosen because admitting I chose them made the sacrifices mine.”

That is the most complicated thing Mom has ever said to me.

I am keeping it here until I understand it.
ENTRY 0143 — 2004-09-18 — Seventh Grade Is Louder

Then Monique said, “Congratulations on identifying anatomy,” and kept walking. I followed her.

I laughed later. At the time I wanted to crawl inside my locker. I keep wondering why a compliment can feel like being touched.

He did not touch me. But he took something private and announced it.
ENTRY 0144 — 2004-10-03 — Hockey Night

ENTRY 0151 — 2005-02-11 — Erica Dawson

ENTRY 0152 — 2005-02-27 — Driving Lesson Three

ENTRY 0153 — 2005-03-12 — The Scale at Kelly's

ENTRY 0154 — 2005-03-30 — Dad Caught Me Skipping Lunch

I said food. He said that is not a plan.

Then he told me if I want to change how I eat, we can learn what actually makes sense, but starving at lunch and eating everything at night is not engineering. His word. He made me sit with him and eat half the stale sandwich anyway.
