ENTRY 0156 — 2005-05-06 — Jason Is Twenty

Jason is twenty. That number sounds ridiculous attached to my brother.

He is working more and baseball matters less now. I think that hurts him even though he acts relieved. At dinner Dad asked what Jason wants to do next.

Jason said he does not know. There was a silence.

For once I felt sorry for him. Baseball gave him a script for years. Practices, tournaments, stats, next season.

Now everybody asks what comes after. Maybe being the celebrated kid has traps too.

I do not need to resent every part of his childhood just because some of it took attention away from me.
ENTRY 0157 — 2005-05-27 — Monique's Birthday

Monique is fifteen today, which means she is almost a full year older than some people in our grade and cares approximately zero percent. I asked if it bothers her. She said, “Why would surviving longer embarrass me?”

I told her I am older because I repeated a grade. She already knew pieces but not the whole story.

I told her about Tasha and Renee. She listened without interrupting.

Then she said, “You did a right thing badly.”

That is almost exactly Dad's sentence, just shorter. I said so. She said my father sounds smart.

He is.
ENTRY 0158 — 2005-06-03 — Seventh Grade Done

Seventh grade is over. I joined academic bowl for next year. I cannot believe I wrote that.

Mr. Feldman looked absurdly pleased.

Shannon says she will come watch if there are snacks. Erica says I should still try cheer eventually. I told her I would rather answer questions about obscure presidents in front of strangers than wear a skirt and jump in front of them.

She said that means I am willing to embarrass myself intellectually but not physically. I hate smart friends.
ENTRY 0159 — 2005-06-18 — Learning Brakes

Dad showed me brake pads today. Not just what they are.

How to tell wear. How the caliper works.

Why you never let it hang by the hose. Why you clean everything before putting it back together. He made me do one side while he watched.

I asked three times if the car was going to kill us afterward. He said, “Not if you torque what I tell you to torque.”

We tested carefully.

It stopped. That feeling — doing something important with my own hands and then seeing it work — is better than almost anything.
ENTRY 0160 — 2005-07-04 — Fireworks and Boys

Fireworks again. The Barracuda hood is getting too familiar as a seat. A boy from school named Caleb came over and talked to me before dark.

He asked about the car. I told him more than he wanted to know about the 318. He did not leave.

That surprised me. Then he asked if the Heart tape was mine or Dad's.

I said both. He said his sister likes Heart. This is not romance.

I know that. I still replayed it in bed.

Fourteen is humiliating.
ENTRY 0161 — 2005-07-22 — Mom's Grocery Math

Mom taught me how she plans groceries. Not coupons exactly.

A whole system. What is cheapest per ounce. What can become two meals.

What freezes. What the boys will inhale before Tuesday.

What is a “false bargain” because nobody actually eats it. I asked why she never taught Jason this. She looked at me.

I looked at her. We both knew the answer was probably because I am a girl.

She sighed and said, “You're right. I should have.”

I appreciate when Mom admits things without turning it into a defense of 1985. I am going to make my brothers learn how much milk costs.
ENTRY 0162 — 2005-08-08 — Eighth Grade Shopping

I bought jeans one size smaller. I am not dieting exactly, but walking with Mom and being more active has changed me some. The number made me happy. That worries me.

I want to be happy because I feel stronger, not because a label approves of me. Still, I stood in the fitting room and turned sideways and smiled.

Both things can be true. That sentence is becoming annoying because it applies to everything.
ENTRY 0163 — 2005-08-24 — Eighth Grade

First day. I am at the top of middle school now. The sixth graders look tiny.

I wonder if I looked that tiny even with my body doing everything early. Academic bowl meets next week.

My stomach already hurts. Erica signed up for cheer clinic. She keeps telling me to come watch.

I might. Watching is not trying.

This is how I trick myself into things.
ENTRY 0164 — 2005-09-09 — Academic Bowl

First academic bowl practice. I answered six questions correctly and missed four.

The world did not end when I missed. Monique was right.

One question was about the Missouri Compromise. I knew it from a book I read for no reason last summer. The coach said, “Mercer, where have you been hiding?”

I said, “Mostly the library.”

Everyone laughed, but not at me.

I liked it. That is dangerous.
ENTRY 0165 — 2005-09-27 — Erica's Cheer Practice

I watched cheer practice. They work much harder than I expected. I thought cheer was smiling and yelling.

It is running, jumps, stretching, timing, strength, remembering routines while breathing hard. Erica came over afterward sweaty and red-faced and said, “Still think we're decorative?”

I admitted I did. She said I have strong legs and should try conditioning with them sometime.

I said no. She said, “That's not a permanent answer.”

She is annoyingly confident about my future.
ENTRY 0166 — 2005-10-14 — Caleb Asked Me Out

Caleb asked if I wanted to go to the fall festival with him. I said, “Like together?”

Excellent, Tammy.

He said, “Yeah, that's generally what asking means.”

I laughed because otherwise I might have died. I said yes. Then I spent the entire afternoon wanting to undo it.

Mom says nervous and wrong are not the same feeling. I am trying to remember that.
ENTRY 0167 — 2005-10-22 — The Fall Festival

I went with Caleb. It was not a disaster. It was not magical either. We rode two rides, shared fries, and talked about music.

He kept trying to win me a stuffed bear and failed spectacularly. At the end he kissed me.

My first kiss. It was quick and soft and a little crooked because I turned my head wrong. I felt electricity for approximately half a second and then became aware of my nose.

I came home smiling anyway. I did not tell Dad.

Mom knew immediately. She said nothing. She just smiled into the refrigerator like an infuriating person.
ENTRY 0168 — 2005-11-06 — Fifteen

I am fifteen. I have now been kissed once. Apparently this is the statistic my brain cares about today.

Caleb gave me a CD he burned with songs he likes. I gave him a book he may never read.

We are not officially anything because neither of us has said the word boyfriend. I am okay with that. Dad gave me a real set of combination wrenches for my birthday.

I may love the wrenches more than the CD. That probably explains me better than I want it to.
ENTRY 0169 — 2005-11-20 — Not a Boyfriend

Caleb and I stopped whatever we were doing. No fight.

He likes a girl in ninth grade. He told me instead of just disappearing, which I respect.

I cried in the shower anyway. It was one kiss and three weeks of talking. Why does my body react like somebody died?

Shannon says first things are allowed to hurt out of proportion because there is nothing older to compare them to. That is smart.

I told her so. She said, “I know.”
ENTRY 0170 — 2005-12-03 — Cheer Conditioning

I went to cheer conditioning with Erica. Do not panic. I did not join anything.

I nearly died during sprints. Then we did squats, jumps, stretches, and something called mountain climbers that should be illegal. I liked it.

Not every second. But afterward my whole body felt awake.

Nobody laughed at me. Erica corrected my form once and then moved on. I am going back next week.

I have not told Mom because she will make A Face.
ENTRY 0171 — 2005-12-15 — Shannon Is Fifteen

Shannon turned fifteen. She knows about cheer conditioning now and says this proves Erica has been running a long con.

We went for Chinese food with her family. I got beef and broccoli and stole two crab rangoons from her plate. We talked about high school next year.

She is excited. I am terrified.

I told her I worry we will become different people. She said, “We already are different people. That's why we're friends, idiot.”

Fair.
ENTRY 0172 — 2006-01-02 — Running Shoes

I bought running shoes with Christmas money. I have been doing cheer conditioning twice a week and walking on other days. I am losing some weight. People have started noticing.

Aunt Denise said, “Look at you getting so pretty.”

I know she meant well. I wanted to ask if I was ugly in October.

I did not. Compliments can carry old insults inside them.
ENTRY 0173 — 2006-01-19 — Dad's Rule About Parts

Dad caught me looking at an advertisement for a carburetor kit online. I said the Barracuda might run better with it. He asked what evidence I had that the carburetor was the problem.

I had none. He said, “Never buy a part because you're tired of not knowing.”

That is one of his rules.

Diagnose first. I think people could use that rule too. We replace explanations too quickly.
ENTRY 0174 — 2006-02-03 — A Different Kind of Comment

A boy who used to call me “big Tammy” told me I look good now. Now.

I said, “I looked like me before too.”

He blinked. I walked away before he could decide whether I had thanked him.

I was proud of myself for about ten minutes. Then I went to the bathroom and checked my stomach in the mirror. Progress is not a straight line.
ENTRY 0175 — 2006-02-14 — No Valentine Drama

No boyfriend this Valentine's Day. I survived. Shannon and I exchanged candy.

Monique gave everybody pencils with hearts because she said romance should at least improve school supplies. Caleb said hi in the hallway and it did not hurt. That feels like something.
ENTRY 0176 — 2006-03-01 — Cheer Tryout Form

Erica handed me a high-school cheer tryout form. I laughed.

She did not. I said I cannot tumble. She said not everybody can.

I said I am not built like a cheerleader. She said, “You've been conditioning with us for four months.

At what point are you going to notice you are literally doing the work?”

I had no answer. I brought the form home. It is under my mattress like contraband.
ENTRY 0177 — 2006-03-12 — Mom Found the Form

Mom found the cheer form while changing sheets. I accused her of snooping. She said, “Tammy, it was under a fitted sheet. I did not conduct a raid.”

She asked why I had not told her.

I said because I probably will not try out. She said, “Then why hide it?”

Again with the questions.

Dad signed the parent line tonight. He said, “Trying out is not agreeing to become somebody else.”

I cried afterward, which is ridiculous. Maybe I am scared I could actually make it.

Failure is easier to imagine than change.
ENTRY 0178 — 2006-03-28 — Grandpa Walter's Back

Grandpa Walter hurt his back and Dad went over to fix a leaking faucet because Grandpa refuses to admit he cannot crawl under sinks right now. I went too. Grandpa stood over us giving instructions while Dad ignored him.

I replaced the washer. Grandpa said, “Raymond, she has your hands.”

Dad said, “No.

She's got hers.”

I pretended not to hear because I did not want either of them to see what that did to me.
ENTRY 0179 — 2006-04-08 — Tryouts

Cheer tryouts were today. I almost left twice.

The gym smelled like floor wax and nerves. I forgot part of the routine during practice, then remembered it when it mattered.

My jumps are not as high as Erica's. I am strong. I am loud.

I can keep count. Afterward I went home and slept for two hours.

Results Monday. I have decided I do not care. This is a lie.
ENTRY 0180 — 2006-04-10 — I Made It

I made the freshman cheer squad. I read the list three times. My name did not move.

TAMMY MERCER. Erica screamed and hugged me. I started crying in the hallway.

Not graceful tears. Full stupid face.

I called Mom from the office. She screamed too. Dad said, “Knew you could.”

I said, “No you didn't.”

He said, “No.

But I knew you could survive trying.”

That is better.
ENTRY 0181 — 2006-04-19 — April and the Uniform

April saw me trying on the cheer uniform sample. She stared for a second and said, “Holy shit, Tammy.”

Then she slapped both hands over her mouth because Aunt Denise was in the next room.

I laughed. The skirt is shorter than anything I voluntarily wear. The top fits tight.

My first instinct was panic. Then I looked again.

I look like a cheerleader. Not a fake one. Me.

This may be complicated.
ENTRY 0182 — 2006-05-05 — Academic Bowl Last Meet

Last academic bowl meet of middle school. I answered the final question that won our round. It was about photosynthesis. I knew it before the moderator finished reading.

I hit the buzzer. For once I did not wait to be certain enough for everyone else.

The sound of my teammates yelling felt almost like baseball applause. I understand Jason a little more now.
ENTRY 0183 — 2006-05-26 — Middle School Almost Over

We cleaned lockers today. I found an old pad wrapper, three pencils, a petrified granola bar, and the note Caleb wrote me before the fall festival. I kept the note.

Not because I still want him. Because I want proof that my life happened while I was busy being embarrassed by it.

High school starts in August. I am going in as the older girl again. But I am also going in as somebody who made cheer and academic bowl and can change brake pads.

That seems like a decent collection of selves.
ENTRY 0184 — 2006-06-02 — Eighth Grade Finished

Middle school is done. I walked out carrying my yearbook against my chest.

There are signatures from people I barely know saying “stay sweet” and “never change.”

Never change is a terrible wish for a fourteen- or fifteen-year-old. Please change.

Please learn things. Please stop making the same mistakes. Please keep enough of yourself to recognize who did the changing.

That is what I would write if yearbooks allowed paragraphs.
ENTRY 0185 — 2006-06-14 — Mom and Dad's Anniversary

Twenty-two years married. Mom and Dad went to dinner. I asked Mom how she knew he was the one.

She said she did not know in the movie way. She chose him, then kept learning whether the choice could survive real life. I asked if that is romantic.

She said, “Depends how old you are.”

I think it is. Not dramatic.

Romantic. There is a difference.
ENTRY 0186 — 2006-06-30 — Summer Conditioning

Cheer conditioning is four mornings a week now. I am sore everywhere.

My body is changing faster than I expected. My waist is smaller. My legs are stronger.

My face looks different. My chest is still my chest, which means now it looks even more obvious against the rest of me.

I thought losing weight would make me less noticeable. That may have been a serious misunderstanding.
ENTRY 0187 — 2006-07-04 — Barracuda and Barracuda

We drove the Barracuda to fireworks. Heart was playing in the 8-track. Mike yelled “BARRACUDA IN THE BARRACUDA” like he had invented the joke today instead of hearing it for ten years. Dad said family humor is mostly repetition with loyalty.

I drove the last loop around the parking area after everybody left. Dad barely corrected me.

I am getting good. I want my license the second I am allowed.
ENTRY 0188 — 2006-07-18 — The First Real Cheer Uniform

My uniform arrived. I put it on in my bedroom and stared. I have worked hard.

That part is true. I also look more like the girls I used to assume lived in a separate universe.

That feels dangerous. I do not want to become mean because I am prettier. That sounds ridiculous written down.

Pretty is not a moral category. Neither is heavy.

I wish I had understood that at nine.
ENTRY 0189 — 2006-07-29 — Shannon and Me

Shannon and I argued. She said I have been spending all my time with cheer people.

I said she is jealous. The second I said it I wanted it back.

She went quiet. That was worse than yelling. I apologized later.

She said, “I don't care if you make new friends. I care if you start acting like old friends are evidence of who you used to be.”

Ouch.

I promised she is not evidence. She is Shannon. We got Chinese food and split lo mein.

Peace treaty.
ENTRY 0190 — 2006-08-08 — Registration

High school registration. The building smells like wax, paper, and old air-conditioning. I got my schedule.

Honors English. Geometry. Biology.

World history. Cheer practice after school.

I am nervous about everything and weirdly excited. Mom kept touching my shoulder like she wanted to make sure I was still there. Maybe high school feels like a departure to parents too.
ENTRY 0191 — 2006-08-19 — Dad's Advice Before High School

Dad gave me exactly one piece of advice. “Don't borrow trouble.”

I asked what that means.

He said, “If something bad happens, deal with it. Don't live it ten times first just because you can imagine it.”

I said that sounds impossible. He said, “Didn't say easy.”

I am writing it down because my brain specializes in borrowing trouble.
ENTRY 0192 — 2006-08-24 — Fifteen and a Half, Freshman

First day of high school. I am fifteen and will be sixteen in November. Some freshmen are fourteen. The age difference is back in my face again.

Nobody asked why today. Cheer gives me an identity before people know my history.

That is useful and uncomfortable. A senior said “hey, cheerleader” to me in the hall. I looked behind me.

He laughed. Apparently he meant me.
ENTRY 0193 — 2006-08-31 — First Game

First football game cheering. I thought I would be terrified. I was for the first five minutes.

Then the band started, the crowd got loud, and there was too much to remember for self-consciousness. Counts.

Signs. Sideline spacing. Watch the game.

Smile without looking insane. Afterward my throat hurt and my legs shook.

Dad said I looked confident. I said I was faking it. He said, “Most confidence is practiced before it becomes true.”
ENTRY 0194 — 2006-09-08 — The Look

Something has changed. Boys look at me differently.

I know that sounds conceited. It is not always pleasant.

A boy who ignored me in middle school held a door for me today and then stared at my chest. Part of me was pleased he noticed me. I hate admitting that.

Another part wanted to dump my books on his foot. I spent years wanting proof I could become pretty.

Nobody warned me proof would come with strangers acting like they bought a ticket.
ENTRY 0195 — 2006-09-19 — Monique's Rule

Monique says I need a rule. “If somebody only got nicer because you got smaller, that is information.”

She said it after I told her about the door boy. I asked if people are allowed to change.

She said yes, but I am allowed to remember what caused the change. I like that. Information, not necessarily condemnation.

Dad would approve.
ENTRY 0196 — 2006-10-01 — Sunday After a Game

Church feels strange after Friday nights now. Friday: skirt, crowd, boys, music, adrenaline.

Sunday: dress below the knee, hymns, modesty, everybody pretending teenagers do not have bodies. I do not think faith and being in a body should feel like opposite jobs. I have not figured out how to say that without sounding rebellious.

Maybe I am rebellious.
ENTRY 0197 — 2006-10-13 — Homecoming Week

Homecoming week is insane. Every hallway is decorated. Cheer has something every day. I am exhausted and happy.

A sophomore named Brett asked if I am going to the dance. I said with friends.

He said he could change that. I laughed because I did not know what else to do. He is cute.

I am not sure I like him. It is possible to enjoy being asked without wanting the person who asked.

I am learning distinctions.
ENTRY 0198 — 2006-10-21 — Homecoming Dance

I went with Shannon, Erica, and Monique. Brett found me halfway through and asked me to dance. I said yes.

His hands went to my waist. That was fine.

Then lower. I moved them back up. He did it again.

I stepped away. He said, “Relax.”

I said, “I was relaxed until you stopped listening.”

I walked back to my friends.

My heart was pounding. Erica asked if I was okay. I was.

I am proud of myself. I did not freeze.

I did not hit him. I did not negotiate my own discomfort. Dad's lesson is still working in places he probably never imagined.
ENTRY 0199 — 2006-11-01 — Sixteen Is Next Week

I will be sixteen in five days. Driver's license soon if I pass.

Dad has been making me parallel park until I hate geometry. The Barracuda is unforgiving because it is approximately the size of a naval vessel.

I love it anyway. I keep thinking sixteen is supposed to be a magical age. Mostly I want the freedom to drive to the library without asking anyone.

This probably says everything.
ENTRY 0200 — 2006-11-06 — Sixteen

Sixteen. Dad put the Barracuda keys in a small box at breakfast. Not ownership.

Not yet. Permission. He said I can drive it when he says it is mechanically ready and when I prove I am not an idiot.

I asked how long the second part takes. He said, “Ask your brothers.

We're still waiting.”

I passed my written test last week. Road test is coming. Mom gave me a little leather wallet for my license.

Shannon gave me a keychain that says READ BANNED BOOKS. Erica gave me mascara.

Monique gave me a tiny screwdriver set “for when the patriarchy has loose screws.”

My friends are ridiculous. I looked at myself tonight in the bathroom mirror. Cheerleader.

Honors classes. Still curvy.

Still self-conscious. Still the girl who hit Tasha. Still the girl who protected Renee.

Still Dad's garage shadow. Still Mom's daughter.

Still Shannon's weird friend. I used to think changing meant becoming someone else. Maybe it means becoming more fully the same person.

ENTRY 0201 — 2006-11-18 — Road Test

I passed. I have a driver's license.

The examiner had a mustache that made him look permanently disappointed. I hit the curb once while parallel parking and thought I had failed, but apparently one small curb encounter is survivable. Dad waited in the parking lot pretending not to pace.

When I came out holding the paper he nodded like I had completed a weld inspection. Then he smiled.

Mom cried when I got home. She says she did not cry. Her face disagreed.
ENTRY 0202 — 2006-11-22 — First Solo Drive

Dad let me take the Barracuda to the grocery store alone. Alone. I sat behind the wheel for a full minute before starting it because freedom is apparently terrifying. The Heart tape was already in the deck.

I drove under the speed limit the entire way and checked every mirror like the police were grading me. At the store I bought milk, bread, eggs, and a Coke I was not asked to buy.

I parked far away from everyone so nobody could dent the car. When I came back, the Barracuda was sitting there waiting for me. That may be the first time I have understood what adults mean by mobility changing your life.
ENTRY 0203 — 2006-12-02 — Gas Money

Freedom requires gasoline. This is offensive. Dad made a rule: if I am driving for family errands, he pays.

If I am driving because I want to go somewhere, I put gas in the car. I told him I do not have a job.

He said, “Excellent observation.”

So now I am looking for work. Mom says I should not work so many hours that grades or cheer suffer. I said I only need enough for gas and occasional Chinese food.

This is apparently my first personal budget.
ENTRY 0204 — 2006-12-15 — Shannon at Sixteen

Shannon is sixteen. I drove us to Chinese food.

That sentence alone makes this birthday different from the others. We sat in a booth and talked about how weird it is that we can now legally operate several thousand pounds of machinery but still have to ask permission to stay out past eleven.

She says Eric is ancient history. I told her Caleb is too. Then we made a toast with iced tea to “boys who become anecdotes.”
ENTRY 0205 — 2006-12-29 — A Job Application

I filled out my first job application. Riverview Route 3 Diner is hiring weekend bussers and hosts. It is not glamorous.

It smells like coffee, fryer oil, and pie. I like it. A woman named Lisa took my application and asked if I can work hard without getting precious about it.

I said I have two older brothers and a father who welds for a living. She said that was not an answer.

So I said yes. She smiled. I think that was the right answer.
ENTRY 0206 — 2007-01-04 — Hired

I got the diner job. Saturday and Sunday mornings, sometimes one evening.

Minimum wage plus whatever tip-out the servers give bussers. Mom immediately started talking about saving. Dad said first paycheck should contain one stupid purchase so work does not become pure punishment.

Mom stared at him. He said, “One.”

I already know what I want: a better pair of headphones.
ENTRY 0207 — 2007-01-13 — First Shift

My feet hurt. My back hurts. I smell like bacon. I love having a job.

Lisa moves through the diner like she can see five minutes into the future. Coffee low at table six.

Kid about to spill juice at nine. Man at counter getting angry before he speaks. She told me never walk empty-handed if something can be carried where I am going.

That sounds like Dad. Working people have secret laws nobody teaches in school.
ENTRY 0208 — 2007-01-20 — First Paycheck

My first paycheck looked bigger before taxes. I have discovered withholding. Government is now personal.

I bought the headphones anyway. Then I put forty dollars into savings and twenty into the envelope marked GAS.

Mom approved. Dad asked what I learned. I said taxes are theft.

He said, “Wait until you use a road.”
ENTRY 0209 — 2007-02-02 — A Regular Named Earl

There is a man named Earl who comes into the diner every Saturday at 6:20. Not around 6:20.

At 6:20. He drinks coffee black and orders two eggs over medium, wheat toast, no potatoes.

Lisa told me to remember people because being remembered is part of what they are paying for even if it is not on the menu. I thought that sounded sentimental. Then Earl came in and I said, “Coffee?” before he sat down.

His whole face changed. Maybe Lisa knows things.
ENTRY 0210 — 2007-02-11 — Erica's Birthday

Erica is sixteen. Her party had more cheer girls than people I know well. I realized I am one of them now.

That sounds stupid, but there was a time girls like this were a category I observed from the outside. They are not a category. One is insecure about her nose.

One hates math. One has parents getting divorced.

One tells jokes when she is scared. Groups look simple from far away. People do not.
ENTRY 0211 — 2007-02-18 — Brett Again

Brett apologized for homecoming. Months late, but still.

He said he was being an ass and should have listened the first time I moved his hands. I said yes. He looked surprised.

I think he expected me to rescue him from his own apology. I did not.

Then I said thank you for saying it. We are not friends exactly. But I do not hate him.
ENTRY 0212 — 2007-03-03 — Snow Shift

It snowed hard this morning and half the diner staff called out. I worked eight hours. Lisa taught me to run plates because the dishwasher was also missing. I spilled gravy on my shoe, got snapped at by a man who had waited too long for coffee, and made twenty-seven dollars in tip-out.

I came home exhausted and strangely proud. Dad said, “Welcome to earning money when conditions are stupid.”
ENTRY 0213 — 2007-03-16 — The Scale Moved

I am losing weight. Slowly. Cheer practice, diner shifts, walking, and eating differently without starving seem to be doing it.

I can feel it in my uniform before I see it on a scale. People comment now.

Almost every comment makes me happy and mad. I wish nobody had taught me that shrinking deserves applause. I also like the applause.

There is the contradiction again.
ENTRY 0214 — 2007-03-30 — Jason Moved Out

Jason moved into an apartment with two friends. Mom cried after he left.

Then she went into his room and said, “Good Lord, open a window.”

We laughed until she cried again. His room looks enormous empty.

I asked if I can have it because it is bigger. Mom said absolutely not. Worth trying.
ENTRY 0215 — 2007-04-11 — Jason Is Twenty-Two

Jason came over for his birthday dinner. He seems more adult in his own apartment and more twelve the minute he sits at Mom's table. He complained about rent.

Dad said, “You wanted independence.”

Jason said, “I wanted independence with utilities included.”

I am learning that adulthood is mostly invoices.
ENTRY 0216 — 2007-04-19 — April and Me in a Dressing Room

April and I went shopping. She tried on dresses for spring formal.

I tried on one because she bullied me into it. It fit. Really fit.

Waist, hips, chest, everything. I stared so long April asked if I was okay.

I said, “I don't look like the person in my head.”

She understood immediately. That is one of the strangest things about losing weight. The mirror changes faster than the memory of the mirror.
ENTRY 0217 — 2007-05-04 — A Customer Grabbed My Wrist

A drunk customer grabbed my wrist when I tried to take his plate. Not hard enough to hurt. Hard enough to make my whole body go cold. Lisa was beside me before I even pulled away.

She said, “Hands off the staff.”

No smile. No apology in her voice.

He let go and tried to joke. She told him his check was ready. Afterward she said I never have to be nice to someone who is touching me without permission.

I knew that already. It still helped hearing an adult woman say it at work.
ENTRY 0218 — 2007-05-18 — Freshman Finals

Finals. Geometry tried to kill me but failed. I got an A-minus.

Honors English A. Biology A.

World history A. Dad put the report card on the refrigerator. Mike said there is barely room for food because of my academic propaganda.

I told him intelligence is shelf-stable.
ENTRY 0219 — 2007-05-31 — Freshman Year Finished

Freshman year is over. I am still here.

No giant disaster. Cheer went well.

Grades are good. Job is good. Shannon is still Shannon.

Monique is still smarter than everybody and announces it less than she could. I have lost enough weight that people who have known me for years sometimes look twice.

That is becoming a story I do not yet know how to tell.
ENTRY 0220 — 2007-06-10 — Lisa's Rule About Tips

Lisa says tips are not compliments. “They're income,” she said. “Don't confuse being charming with being owed respect.”

She said this after a man left one of the servers twenty dollars and his phone number.

I asked if that was bad. Lisa said the money spends fine. The phone number can go in the trash.

I like her.
ENTRY 0221 — 2007-06-23 — Driving Dad to the Parts Store

Dad let me drive while he sat in the passenger seat and criticized my braking. He says I brake like I am negotiating with the pedal.

I say he drives like stop signs are personal suggestions. We bought belts and a radiator hose. The man at the counter answered Dad when I asked a question.

Dad said, “She asked you.”

The man looked at me then. I answered my own question before he could.

Dad did not smile until we got outside.
ENTRY 0222 — 2007-07-04 — Freedom Is Gasoline

I drove Shannon to fireworks. No parents in the car. We played the Heart tape too loud and sang badly. I paid for gas with diner money.

I thought about how many little freedoms are purchased in five-dollar increments.
ENTRY 0223 — 2007-07-20 — Cheer Camp

Cheer camp. Three days of sore muscles, bad cafeteria food, and learning routines until my brain turned into counts. I am stronger than last year.

A coach complimented my jumps. Nobody called me the heavy girl trying to keep up.

I do not know if anyone ever thought that besides me. Sometimes I am the cruelest person in the room to myself.
ENTRY 0224 — 2007-08-03 — The Uniform Fits Differently

Uniform fitting. Last year's top is loose at the waist.

My chest still fills it completely. Erica said, “Okay, boobs, we get it,” while helping pin the side.

I laughed because it was Erica and because she said it to my face like a joke between friends, not a public announcement. Context changes everything. I wish boys understood context.
ENTRY 0225 — 2007-08-20 — Sophomore Year

First day sophomore year. I know the building now. That alone makes everything easier.

A freshman asked me where the science wing is. I gave directions and realized I have become one of the older people who knows things. Not old.

Older. I am sixteen until November.
ENTRY 0226 — 2007-08-31 — The First Nickname

Someone called me “Jugs” today. I am writing the word because I refuse to let it become more powerful by pretending I did not hear it.

A junior boy said it while I was walking in uniform with Erica. He did not even know my name. Erica turned around and said, “Her name is Tammy.”

He laughed.

I kept walking. I spent the rest of the day wanting to put on my biggest sweatshirt.

I spent years being mocked for being fat. Now I am smaller and they found a new body part to turn into public property. What exactly was I supposed to win?
ENTRY 0227 — 2007-09-02 — Mom's Birthday

Mom is forty-three. I told her about “Jugs.”

I did not mean to. It came out while we were doing dishes. She stopped washing.

Her face changed. She said, “I am sorry.”

Not “ignore them.”

Not “boys are boys.”

Just sorry.

Then she said I can wear whatever makes me comfortable but she does not want some stupid boy choosing my clothes for me from a distance. I wore the uniform Friday. I hated that she was right.
ENTRY 0228 — 2007-09-14 — It Spread

The nickname spread. Not everywhere. Enough.

Usually muttered. Sometimes shouted.

The worst part is that some girls laugh too. I know why. If they laugh with the boys, the spotlight stays on me.

I understand it and still resent them. Monique says I should start assigning nicknames back based on personality defects.

We made a list in study hall. That helped more than it should have.
ENTRY 0229 — 2007-09-28 — A Teacher Heard

Mr. Daniels heard a boy call me Jugs in the hallway.

He stopped everything. Made the boy come back.

Made him use my actual name and apologize. I wanted to disappear during the entire thing. Afterward Mr.

Daniels asked if I wanted him to report it formally. I said no.

Then I wondered if that was cowardly. Maybe I just wanted one day where my chest did not generate paperwork.
ENTRY 0230 — 2007-10-06 — Homecoming Dress

I bought a homecoming dress. Dark blue. Not high-necked, not ridiculous, just fitted.

When I put it on I liked myself. That sentence is so rare I am giving it its own paragraph. I liked myself.
ENTRY 0231 — 2007-10-13 — Homecoming

Went with friends again. A guy named Trevor danced with me and did not touch me anywhere I did not want.

The bar is apparently underground. He told me I have pretty eyes. I nearly asked if he had noticed them above the rest of me.

I did not. Sometimes receiving a normal compliment should be allowed to be normal.
ENTRY 0232 — 2007-10-27 — Jason Saw the Nickname Happen

Jason picked me up after a game because Dad was working late. Some boys yelled the nickname as I crossed the parking lot. Jason got out of the truck. I said, “Do not.”

He looked at me.

I said it again. He got back in.

The whole drive home he gripped the steering wheel like it owed him money. Finally he said, “I didn't know.”

I said, “You didn't ask.”

That was mean. Also true.
ENTRY 0233 — 2007-11-06 — Seventeen

Seventeen. I worked after school, then came home to cake. Dad gave me a proper socket set with metric and SAE.

Mom gave me a coat that actually has a waist. Shannon gave me a used copy of a psychology book from a college bookstore because she said I keep reading about why people do things.

I started it tonight. The first chapter says behavior is influenced by biology, learning, thought, and social context. Apparently the answer to why people do things is “everything.”
ENTRY 0234 — 2007-11-18 — Psychology

I cannot stop reading the psychology book. There are studies about conformity where people give obviously wrong answers because everybody else does.

That makes school make more sense. It also makes church uncomfortable to think about.

I told Mr. Cline that. He said faith that cannot survive questions is not much use.

I respect him for saying that.
ENTRY 0235 — 2007-11-29 — Weight Goal

I reached the number I once wrote secretly as my “goal weight.”

I expected trumpets. Nothing happened. My thighs still look like my thighs.

My chest is still large. I still find flaws. This is useful information.

The problem was never only the number.
ENTRY 0236 — 2007-12-08 — Diner Christmas Rush

The diner is insane in December. People shopping come in tired and mean.

Lisa says hunger turns personality volume up. A woman snapped at me because her pie was cold. I wanted to snap back.

Instead I heated it, brought fresh coffee, and she apologized before she left. I felt powerful in a completely different way from winning an argument.
ENTRY 0237 — 2007-12-15 — Shannon Is Seventeen

Shannon is seventeen. We have known each other long enough to have versions of each other nobody else remembers. She remembers me before cheer. Before weight loss.

Before boys started looking. I asked if I seem different.

She said, “More confident. Also more suspicious when somebody says you're pretty.”

Fair.
ENTRY 0238 — 2007-12-24 — Christmas Eve Service

Christmas Eve service. Candlelight makes everybody look kinder. I sat between Mom and Dad and thought about how safe their shoulders feel on either side of me.

I am seventeen and sometimes desperate to get away from this trailer, this town, these rules. Then a night like this happens and leaving sounds impossible.

I suppose both things can be love.
ENTRY 0239 — 2008-01-01 — No Resolution

No diet resolution again. I am keeping that tradition.

I want to keep grades up, save money, get stronger, and maybe take a psychology class if school offers one next year. I also want to stop thinking every person who looks at me is judging me.

That last one may be harder than geometry.
ENTRY 0240 — 2008-01-09 — Lisa Is Thirty-Six

Lisa is thirty-six today. I asked if thirty-six feels old. She said, “Only when a seventeen-year-old asks me that.”

I apologized.

She laughed. She has two kids and an ex-husband she describes as “a recurring administrative problem.”

She told me not to build my whole life around being chosen by a man. I said I am not.

She said, “Good. Keep not.”
ENTRY 0241 — 2008-01-21 — A College Catalog

A guidance counselor gave me college catalogs. College has always been the vague thing smart kids are supposed to do.

Now there are prices attached. Huge prices. I brought the catalogs home and watched Mom's face as she turned pages.

We did not say “we cannot afford this.”

We did not have to. Dad said community college first is not failure.

I said I know. I did not know until he said it.
ENTRY 0242 — 2008-02-02 — Scholarship Search

I spent three hours looking at scholarships. Some require essays about leadership. Some want service hours. Some are for people whose parents went to college, which is interesting because that seems like the group who needs less help, not more.

Money has architecture. There are doors you only notice when you are the one without a key.
ENTRY 0243 — 2008-02-11 — Erica Is Seventeen

Erica's birthday. She has started talking about college cheer programs. I told her I do not know if I want cheer after high school.

She said I do not have to turn every thing I am good at into a lifelong identity. That was a relief.

Cheer gave me something. It does not have to own me.
ENTRY 0244 — 2008-02-17 — Danny Cole

I talked to Danny Cole today for the first time for more than thirty seconds. Everyone knows who he is.

Junior quarterback. Same age as me, technically, but a grade ahead because of my repeated year.

He came into the diner with two guys after practice. I expected athlete behavior. Loud.

Entitled. Whatever.

He ordered a burger, said please, and stacked the plates when they were done. When I brought coffee for his friend, he noticed the psychology book under the register and asked if it was mine. I said yes.

He asked if it was any good. I said, “Depends how comfortable you are finding out your brain lies to you.”

He smiled.

Then Lisa called me away. That is all. I have written six paragraphs about all.
ENTRY 0245 — 2008-02-29 — Leap Day

Danny came in again. I am not making this a thing. He remembered the book.

Asked what chapter I was on. I said memory. He said football coaches would prefer players forget mistakes immediately.

I told him that is not how memory works. He said, “Then maybe you should come explain that to Coach.”

I laughed.

He has very blue eyes. This is objective data.
ENTRY 0246 — 2008-03-08 — Shannon Knows

Shannon knows I am interested in Danny. I did not tell her.

Apparently I have a face. She said, “The quarterback?”

I said, “He is also a person.”

She said, “Oh God, it's already serious.”

It is not serious. He has spoken to me twice.

Three times if you count asking for ketchup.
ENTRY 0247 — 2008-03-15 — Dad Knows Nothing

Dad mentioned Danny during the game on TV because there was a local sports segment. I nearly dropped a fork. Dad noticed. I said nothing.

He said nothing. The silence had eyebrows.

I hate fathers.
ENTRY 0248 — 2008-03-28 — Danny at School

Danny said hi to me in the hallway. In front of people. That sounds pathetic written down.

But there is a difference between a boy talking to the waitress at his diner and the quarterback saying “Hey, Tammy” while walking with teammates. He knows my name outside the building.

I am seventeen. I am allowed one stupid entry.
ENTRY 0249 — 2008-04-04 — Cheer Practice with a Different Body

I realized today I can do a jump I could not do last year. My body feels capable now in ways that have nothing to do with being thinner.

That matters. I want to remember the strength more than the size.
ENTRY 0250 — 2008-04-11 — Jason Is Twenty-Three

Jason is twenty-three. He brought a woman named Melissa to dinner. She is funny and not impressed by him.

I like her immediately. She asked me about school, not cheer. Jason told her I can rebuild half a car.

I said that is an exaggeration. Dad said, “For now.”

I glowed for the rest of dinner.
ENTRY 0251 — 2008-04-19 — April's Eighteenth

April is eighteen. An adult.

We sat on her bed after everyone left and talked about sex. Not details. Fear, mostly.

She said everyone acts like you either do it and become worldly or do not and stay pure, and real feelings are not that tidy. I told her church makes me feel like desire itself is suspicious.

She said desire is not a felony. That is very April. I am not eighteen yet.

I am filing this away.
ENTRY 0252 — 2008-04-27 — Danny Asked About Heart

Danny saw the Barracuda in the school parking lot and asked if it was mine. I said technically Dad's. He looked inside and saw the 8-track. He said, “Does that thing work?”

I said, offended, “Yes.”

I put the Heart tape in.

He laughed at the track-change clunk, not meanly. Then he said the car fits me.

I asked what that means. He said, “Looks tough until you notice somebody takes really good care of it.”

I have been thinking about that for six hours.
ENTRY 0253 — 2008-05-03 — The Number

Danny asked for my number. My phone number. I made him clarify because I am apparently determined to humiliate myself at every milestone.

He laughed and said, “Yes, Mercer, your phone number.”

I gave it to him. Then I immediately worried my handwriting was ugly.

This is how insanity begins.
ENTRY 0254 — 2008-05-05 — He Called

Danny called. The house landline rang because I gave him that number instead of my cheap cell, which has terrible minutes.

Dad answered. Of course Dad answered.

He yelled, “Tammy, Daniel Cole for you,” with the full name like he was announcing a defendant. I wanted the floor to open. Danny was laughing when I picked up.

We talked forty minutes. Books.

Music. Football. His mother.

My diner job. He asked what I want to study.

I said maybe psychology. He said, “That makes sense. You watch people.”

He noticed.
ENTRY 0255 — 2008-05-10 — First Date with Danny

Danny took me to get Mexican food. Nothing fancy. Thank God.

He picked me up and shook Dad's hand. Dad did not crush his fingers, which shows restraint. Danny asked me questions and actually listened to the answers.

We argued about whether athletes are unfairly stereotyped as stupid. I said sometimes athletes help the stereotype by acting stupid.

He said fair. After dinner we walked around for a while. He kissed me by the car.

Not my first kiss. It felt like the first one that mattered.
ENTRY 0256 — 2008-05-16 — Finals and Danny

Studying is harder with a boyfriend-shaped thought occupying part of my brain. Are we boyfriend and girlfriend?

We have not formally declared anything. He calls every night. He kissed me again after school.

I think that counts. Monique says labels are useful for food and relationships because both can spoil if nobody knows what they are.
ENTRY 0257 — 2008-05-23 — He Said Girlfriend

Danny called me his girlfriend. Casually. To somebody else. “My girlfriend Tammy works at Riverview.”

I pretended not to react.

Inside, fireworks. This is ridiculous and wonderful.
ENTRY 0258 — 2008-05-30 — Sophomore Year Done

Sophomore year finished. A year ago I was still surprised when people thought I was pretty. Now I have a boyfriend, a cheer uniform, a job, good grades, a driver's license, and a brain that still wakes up some mornings convinced I am the fat girl everybody is laughing at.

External evidence changes faster than internal weather.
ENTRY 0259 — 2008-06-07 — Danny Meets Shannon Properly

Danny, Shannon, and I got burgers after my shift. Shannon interrogated him politely.

Favorite band. Favorite class.

Why football. Whether he reads. He answered everything.

Afterward she said, “I approve provisionally.”

I told Danny. He said he feels honored.

Smart man.
ENTRY 0260 — 2008-06-14 — Parents' Anniversary

Twenty-four years for Mom and Dad. Danny asked me what their marriage is like. I said durable.

He said that is not a romantic word. I said it is if you have watched people keep choosing each other when there is no soundtrack. He got quiet.

Then he kissed my forehead. I am in trouble.
ENTRY 0261 — 2008-06-21 — Danny's Truck

Danny has an old pickup that smells like grass, football gear, and whatever air freshener boys buy when they panic. We sat in it after a movie and talked for an hour.

He told me scouts are looking at him. Texas schools among others. He said it like he was trying not to hope too hard.

I told him he is allowed to want things. He said, “So are you.”

That landed somewhere deep.
ENTRY 0262 — 2008-06-29 — The Diner Sees Everything

Lisa knows I am in love before I do. She said, “You have that look.”

I said what look. She said, “Like your future has a face.”

I told her to shut up. She laughed.

I am not in love. Probably.
ENTRY 0263 — 2008-07-04 — Fireworks with Danny

Danny came with us to fireworks. He sat on the Barracuda hood beside me. Dad allowed this, which feels significant.

Mike made the Heart/Barracuda joke. Danny laughed anyway.

Good sign. During the fireworks Danny held my hand. I looked over and saw Dad watching the sky, Mom leaning against him.

For one stupid beautiful minute I could imagine this exact scene years from now with Danny still beside me. That scares me.
ENTRY 0264 — 2008-07-12 — Dad Watches Danny

Dad asked me if Danny treats me well. I said yes.

He asked if he listens when I say no. Dad nodded.

That was the whole conversation. I knew what he meant. I also knew he trusted me enough not to ask for details.

That trust feels heavier than rules.
ENTRY 0265 — 2008-07-18 — Cheer Camp and Missing Him

Cheer camp again. I missed Danny stupidly. Three days.

I am embarrassed by myself. He left a message on my phone saying he hopes I am “jumping cheerleaderly.”

I saved it. I hate myself.
ENTRY 0266 — 2008-07-27 — The Scholarship Talk

Danny says Texas Tech is serious. A coach called.

His parents are trying not to act excited. He is going to visit. I am proud of him.

I am terrified. He is a year ahead in school even though we are the same age.

I have always hated being held back. Tonight I hated it for a new reason. If I had not repeated third grade, we would graduate together.

That thought is unfair to everybody involved, including nine-year-old me. I thought it anyway.
ENTRY 0267 — 2008-08-04 — Junior-Year Schedule

Registration. Junior year. Advanced English, chemistry, U.S. history, algebra II, psychology elective.

Finally psychology. Cheer.

Diner weekends. Danny is a senior. That last fact is the loudest one.
ENTRY 0268 — 2008-08-16 — Danny Back from Texas

Danny loved Texas Tech. He tried to sound neutral. He failed.

He brought me a red T-shirt. I put it on and looked in the mirror and felt like I was trying on his future.

I hate that sentence. It is true.
ENTRY 0269 — 2008-08-25 — Junior Year Begins

First day junior year. Danny found me before first period and kissed me quickly by my locker.

A freshman stared. I wanted to tell her not to build a mythology around people she sees in hallways.

I probably would not have listened at fourteen either.
ENTRY 0270 — 2008-08-29 — First Game as His Girlfriend

First football game. Cheering while Danny plays is completely different. Every hit feels personal.

Every good play makes me stupidly proud. People scream his name. I know what he sounds like when he is tired on the phone.

Both versions are him. I like knowing the private one.
ENTRY 0271 — 2008-09-02 — Mom and Danny

Mom likes Danny. She pretends she is neutral.

She made extra pie because he was coming over. I accused her. She said, “I made pie because we had apples.”

We did not have apples until she bought them for pie.

Case closed.
ENTRY 0272 — 2008-09-09 — Psychology Class

Psychology class is everything I hoped. We talked about attachment today. How early relationships can shape expectations without dictating them. I immediately started diagnosing everyone I know.

Then the teacher warned us not to do that. Too late.

Danny says dating a psychology student sounds dangerous. I said only if he has secrets.
ENTRY 0273 — 2008-09-19 — The Nickname Dies a Little

Somebody called me Jugs at the game. Danny heard. He did not punch anyone.

Thank God. He turned and said, “Her name is Tammy.”

Exactly what Erica said a year ago.

The boy shrugged. Danny came back to me and did not make it a heroic event. That meant more.

I do not need a man to defend me. I do like being beside someone who knows my name matters.
ENTRY 0274 — 2008-09-27 — College Scouts

There were scouts at the game. Danny played well.

Afterward everybody crowded him. I stood back.

For the first time I saw the machinery that might carry him away. Coaches. Statistics.

Adults with clipboards. His father glowing.

I am proud. I am afraid of being selfish. I am afraid of being left.

These are not the same fear.
ENTRY 0275 — 2008-10-03 — Amanda Reeves Exists Somewhere

This entry is ridiculous because I do not know Amanda Reeves. I heard Danny mention a girl named Amanda from a recruiting visit group chat or something. That is all.

I felt jealous. Over a name. I did not say anything because I refuse to become a person who interrogates men about women they have merely mentioned.

Writing it here instead. Dear future Tammy: you were seventeen and insane.
ENTRY 0276 — 2008-10-11 — Homecoming with Danny

Danny took me to homecoming. My dress was green.

Mom adjusted the straps twice. Dad took too many pictures. Danny looked unfairly good in a suit.

We danced until my feet hurt. He kept his hands where I wanted them.

That should not still be noteworthy. It is. Afterward we sat in his truck and kissed for a long time.

I wanted more closeness than I was ready to explain. I went home with my head buzzing.
ENTRY 0277 — 2008-10-18 — Desire

Church taught about sexual purity tonight. Excellent timing. I sat there thinking about Danny's mouth and felt like God had installed a spotlight over my seat. I believe sex matters.

I believe choices matter. I also think pretending teenagers do not feel desire is a terrible teaching strategy.

Wanting is not deciding. I am starting to understand that distinction before I have language for it.
ENTRY 0278 — 2008-10-25 — Dad's Question

Dad asked what time I got home last night. I said 10:58. Curfew eleven.

He said, “Cutting it close.”

I said, “Still legal.”

He said, “This isn't court.”

Then he looked at me and asked if I am okay with how serious things are getting with Danny. I wanted to disappear.

I said yes. He said, “Okay doesn't mean obligated.”

I told him I know. I do.
ENTRY 0279 — 2008-11-01 — One Week to Eighteen, Almost

My birthday is five days away. Eighteen.

Legally adult. The number feels absurd.

I still live in a trailer with my parents. I still ask permission to stay out late. I still forget to move laundry to the dryer.

But the law will decide I am suddenly responsible for all of me. Maybe adulthood is not a feeling.

Maybe it is a jurisdiction.
ENTRY 0280 — 2008-11-06 — Eighteen

I am eighteen. Adult. I wrote it twice and nothing happened.

Dad gave me a torque wrench. Mom gave me a necklace that belonged to Grandma Ruth when she was young. Danny gave me a small silver bracelet with a tiny blue stone.

He said he wanted something I could wear every day without it being “a giant football-boy statement.”

I love him. There.

I wrote it. I have thought it for weeks. Tonight I told him.

He said it back. I believed him.
ENTRY 0281 — 2008-11-08 — After Saying It

Saying I love you changes the air. Not the relationship exactly.

The air. Everything feels more important because there is now a sentence attached. I keep touching the bracelet.

Mom noticed. She asked if I am happy.

I said yes. She said, “Good. Stay awake inside happy.”

I think she means do not confuse happiness with certainty.

Mothers speak in riddles when daughters are in love.
ENTRY 0282 — 2008-11-15 — Texas Tech Offer

Danny got the offer. Texas Tech. Real scholarship. He called me before telling half his friends.

He was laughing and almost crying. I screamed.

Then after we hung up I sat on my bed and cried. Pride and grief at the same time. I am becoming excellent at contradictory feelings.
ENTRY 0283 — 2008-11-22 — Celebration Dinner

Danny's parents had people over to celebrate. Everyone kept saying Texas. Next year.

Football. Opportunity.

Future. I smiled until my face hurt. An aunt asked me what I am going to do “when he goes away.”

I said, “Finish high school.”

She laughed like I made a joke.

I did not.
ENTRY 0284 — 2008-11-27 — Thanksgiving at Eighteen

Thanksgiving. Danny came for dessert after his family dinner.

Grandpa Walter asked him football questions. Grandma June fed him pie until he surrendered.

I watched him fit into my family too easily. That should make me happy. It does.

It also makes the possibility of losing him feel larger.
ENTRY 0285 — 2008-12-05 — Snow in His Truck

It snowed while Danny and I sat in his truck after a movie. We kissed until the windows fogged. He touched me over my clothes and stopped every time I moved his hand.

I wanted him. I was also scared by how much I wanted him. We did not go farther.

He did not pout. He just rested his forehead against mine and said, “We're okay.”

I believed that too.
ENTRY 0286 — 2008-12-15 — Shannon Is Eighteen

Shannon is eighteen. I told her Danny and I are getting more physical.

ENTRY 0287 — 2008-12-24 — Christmas Eve and Guilt

Church tonight. I felt guilty during communion because I have been making out with Danny and thinking about sex. Then I felt guilty for thinking God is keeping a spreadsheet of where Danny's hands have been. Faith is getting harder as I get older because the questions have bodies now.

I still believe. I just do not know whether every rule I inherited is the same thing as God.
ENTRY 0288 — 2009-01-02 — New Year with a Countdown

2009. Danny graduates this year. That sentence sat under midnight like a second clock.

We kissed at twelve. Everybody cheered.

ENTRY 0290 — 2009-01-17 — Acceptance Letter, Sort Of

Danny officially committed. The local paper ran a small story. His picture was in it.

I bought three copies like a crazy person. One for his scrapbook, one for me, one because the first two suddenly seemed insufficient. Dad saw them and said nothing.

He is very kind sometimes.
ENTRY 0291 — 2009-01-24 — A Fight About Texas

Danny and I had our first real fight. I asked how often he thinks we will see each other after he leaves.

He said he does not know. I said he should know. He said how could he know something that has not happened.

I accused him of already planning to leave me behind. He got angry.

Then I got angrier because he was angry. We both said stupid things. Later he came over and we talked on the steps in the cold.

He said, “I want us. I also want Texas.

I don't know how both works yet.”

That is honest. Honesty is not always comforting.
ENTRY 0292 — 2009-02-07 — Nine Years Since the Fight

Nine years today. Tasha. Renee. Blood on my hand.

I almost forgot the date until Mom mentioned it indirectly because she found old school papers. Nine years.

The girl who did that is still in me. So is the girl who learned to stop. I wonder where Tasha is.

I hope she is okay. That surprises me.
ENTRY 0293 — 2009-02-14 — Valentine's Day

Danny brought flowers to the diner. Lisa made gagging noises behind the counter. He also gave me a used book he found because I had mentioned wanting it.

The book mattered more than flowers. He knows that.

We kissed in the Barracuda later with the Heart tape clicking between tracks. I thought: remember this. Not because I expect it to end.

Because I finally understand that good things deserve memory too.
ENTRY 0294 — 2009-02-17 — Danny Is Nineteen

Danny is nineteen. Same age as I will be in November.

He joked that he is now “an older man.”

I reminded him he is only one grade ahead because I punched somebody into administrative consequences. He knows the full story now.

He never looked at me differently after I told him. That is one of the reasons I love him.
ENTRY 0295 — 2009-03-01 — Church and the Future

Mr. Cline asked seniors what they want from adulthood. I am not a senior, but I listened.

Marriage. Career. Family.

Service. Faith.

Nobody said money even though money decides so much. Nobody said sex even though half the room was thinking about it. Nobody said fear.

Adults love clean nouns. Life seems to happen in the messy spaces between them.
ENTRY 0296 — 2009-03-14 — Danny's Hand

Danny and I were alone at his house for a little while today. His parents were out and his sister was supposed to be back soon.

We kissed on the couch and things got intense quickly. He touched my breasts over my shirt, then under it after I moved closer instead of away. I wanted it.

That matters because I keep trying to divide desire into allowed and forbidden before I even feel it. When I got nervous, I said stop.

He stopped. Immediately. No argument.

We sat there breathing like we had run somewhere. I cried a little from embarrassment.

He held my hand and did not ask me to explain.
ENTRY 0297 — 2009-03-20 — I Keep Thinking About It

I keep thinking about Danny touching me. Not because something bad happened. Because I liked it. That is harder for me to admit than fear.

Fear fits the rules I grew up with. Wanting does not.

I am eighteen. I am not a child. I am allowed to understand my own body even if I decide there are things I am not ready to do.

Writing that feels rebellious. Maybe it should not.
ENTRY 0298 — 2009-04-03 — The Diner After Midnight

Worked late. A trucker named Sam told Lisa his wife left him. He said it to her while staring into coffee.

She did not give advice. She just said, “I'm sorry, honey.”

He talked for twenty minutes.

After he left I asked why she did not tell him what to do. She said, “Most people know what they should do. Sometimes they need one place where they can say what happened without being managed.”

I wrote that down on a receipt.
ENTRY 0299 — 2009-04-11 — Jason Is Twenty-Four

Jason turned twenty-four. Melissa is definitely serious.

They talk about getting a place together when his lease ends. Mom pretends this is normal and then asks me what Melissa said every five minutes.

I see how families grow by attaching new people slowly. I wonder if Danny will still be attached to ours years from now. I hope so hard it frightens me.
ENTRY 0300 — 2009-04-18 — Spring Before Everything Changes

The trees are green again. Danny graduates next month. Texas is coming whether I approve or not.

I am finishing junior year with good grades. Cheer banquet next week. Diner shifts all weekend.

Dad says the Barracuda needs front-end work before he wants me driving it long distances. Life is ordinary and enormous at the same time.

Danny came by after practice and sat with me on the hood of the car. We did not talk about Texas for once. We talked about a stupid movie, Mike's terrible haircut, and whether hot-and-sour soup should actually hurt.

I wanted to freeze the ordinary part. Not the grand declarations.

Just him beside me, our shoulders touching, Dad's car cooling under us, Heart still caught halfway through an old tape inside.

ENTRY 0301 — 2009-04-24 — Almost Done

I keep seeing a blue pen mark on the side of my hand, which is probably why I am writing instead of sleeping. Cheer practice left my calves shaking.

I still have moments where I look around and wonder how the heavy girl who hid in sweatshirts ended up in this uniform. Then somebody makes a comment about my chest and the distance between those versions of me disappears instantly. What stayed with me was Mom folding a dish towel twice before putting it away.

What stayed with me was the smell of fryer oil caught in my hair. Mike can make me laugh when I am determined not to.

It is infuriating. He said something stupid at dinner and Mom tried not to smile because she was still mad at him. Families are full of alliances that last eleven seconds.

What stayed sharp was a coffee stain drying on a diner receipt. Dad and I have the Barracuda on stands more often than I would like.

It refuses. Probably healthy.
ENTRY 0302 — 2009-05-01 — After the Dinner Rush

A woman counted change for a grilled cheese and looked embarrassed. I pretended not to notice the counting, rang the cheaper side, and brought extra crackers without announcing charity. Poverty is humiliating enough without somebody turning kindness into a performance. I keep seeing a coffee stain drying on a diner receipt, which is probably why I am writing instead of sleeping.

I keep seeing a blue pen mark on the side of my hand. I watched Mom move through the kitchen tonight and realized how many things she does without naming them as work.

ENTRY 0305 — 2009-05-22 — Danny Graduated

Danny graduated tonight. I cheered when his name was called and then cried so suddenly I embarrassed myself. His mother cried too, which saved me from feeling like the only ridiculous person in the bleachers.

He found me afterward in the crowd, still wearing the gown open over his shirt, and hugged me hard enough that the tassel hit my cheek. Everybody kept saying congratulations and Texas Tech and next chapter. I wanted to be generous about all of it.

I am proud of him. I am also furious that being proud of somebody can involve helping them leave you.

Dad shook Danny's hand and said, “Do the work.” Danny said, “Yes, sir.” Mom hugged him. Mike told him not to become insufferable on television. Jason asked about summer workouts.

Then Danny and I got ten quiet minutes behind the gym near the loading doors. He kissed me and said, “We're not ending because I graduated.”

I believed him.

I still do, tonight. But there is a difference between not ending and not changing. I can feel that difference standing just outside the light, waiting for us.

ENTRY 0318 — 2009-07-04 — Fourth of July Before Texas

Fireworks again, almost exactly like last year, except now every good thing has an expiration date stamped on it. Danny sat beside me on the blanket. His hand was warm around mine.

The boys were throwing a football behind us until somebody's mother threatened murder. Dad had a folding chair and complained about people who bring dogs to fireworks.

Mom brought too much food because that is how she handles uncertainty. When the first big shell opened over the field, I remembered last summer thinking I could imagine Danny sitting beside me years from now. I can still imagine it.

That is the problem. He leaves next month.

I asked him whether he is scared. He admitted he is. Not about football.

About walking into a place where nobody knows him yet. I said I am scared of becoming somebody he used to know.

He squeezed my hand and said, “Tammy, you're not disposable.”

I had to look away because that landed somewhere too deep. I wish love came with engineering drawings. Load limits.

Failure points. Inspection intervals.

Something Dad and I could put on jack stands and test before I trust it with my whole weight.

ENTRY 0335 — 2009-08-15 — Texas

He left this morning. I helped his mother carry a box to the truck and then had to go stand by myself because every ordinary object suddenly felt offensive. Laundry basket.

Duffel bag. Cooler. Football cleats.

Things you can pick up and move while my chest felt nailed to the driveway. Danny hugged me last.

His father was trying very hard not to watch us. He said he would call tonight from the hotel. I told him not to call if he was too tired, which was a lie.

I want him to call if he is unconscious. When the truck turned at the end of the street, I stood there until there was nothing left to look at.

I drove home in the Barracuda. The Heart tape was already in the deck. I made it through about half a song before I started crying so hard I had to pull into a grocery-store parking lot.

Dad called my cell. He did not ask why I was parked there.

He just said, “You okay to drive?”

I said not yet. He said, “Then don't.”

So I sat there with the engine off until I could see clearly again.

ENTRY 0362 — 2009-11-06 — Nineteen

I am nineteen. That looks absurd written down. I do not feel nineteen. I feel sixteen, forty, and about nine depending on the hour.

Danny called before class. He remembered the exact time I was born because Mom told him once and apparently he stored it.

That made me stupidly happy. A package arrived later with a Texas Tech sweatshirt, a paperback he thought I would like, and a note that said he misses my face. I wore the sweatshirt all evening even though it was too warm in the house.

At dinner Mom made chocolate cake. Jason and Mike argued about whether nineteen counts as an adult birthday.

Dad said adulthood begins the first time a bill arrives that nobody else is responsible for. I am old enough now that church conversations about purity sound less like warnings about mysterious danger and more like adults pretending desire becomes morally simple if you use enough solemn words. I love Danny.

I miss him. I want him.

I am afraid of losing him. None of those facts cancels another one.

ENTRY 0389 — 2010-03-27 — Senior Prom Is Real Now

Danny called tonight and asked, formally, whether I would let him come home and take me to senior prom. I laughed because we have been talking about it for months, but hearing him actually ask made my stomach flip.

Mom immediately began calculating dresses. Dad immediately began calculating what time Danny would return me.

I am nineteen years old and Dad can still make midnight sound like federal law. The part I am not saying out loud is that I have been thinking about sex. Not abstract sex.

Not church-class sex. Danny and me.

I stopped us before intercourse last year because I was not ready. I was frightened by how much I wanted him and by the feeling that if I crossed that line, something about me would become permanently different. Now I keep coming back to the same thought: I do not want my first time to happen because I got swept away.

I want to choose it. If I choose it, I want to know I chose it with my eyes open.

I think I am getting there.

ENTRY 0398 — 2010-04-24 — What I Told Shannon

I told Shannon what I am thinking about prom. All of it. She did not squeal or lecture me.

She asked whether Danny knows I am thinking this way. I said not exactly.

She asked whether I can tell him no at any point and trust him to stop. I said yes without hesitation. Then she asked the harder question: “If he leaves again two days later and your future still doesn't work out, will you believe you gave him something he stole?”

I sat there for a long time.

No. That answer surprised me by how clear it was.

If I have sex with Danny, I want it to be because I want to be with him, not because I am purchasing permanence. I cannot make him marry me with my body. I cannot make the future hold still.

That does not mean the choice is meaningless. It means the meaning has to exist tonight, in us, not as a contract with ten years from now.

ENTRY 0402 — 2010-05-01 — Senior Prom

Dear Diary,

I had sex with Danny tonight. I am writing that plainly because if I hide from the sentence then I will spend the rest of this page talking around my own life. Prom was beautiful and ridiculous. My feet hurt before dinner was over.

Danny looked older in his suit. We danced badly and laughed.

When he kissed me in the car afterward I knew I was still choosing what I had been thinking about for weeks. We used a condom. He was nervous too, which helped.

The first penetration hurt more than I expected and I made him stop moving. He stopped immediately.

We stayed still, forehead to forehead, until I told him I was okay to continue. It got easier. Not magical.

Not like movies. Awkward, tender, intense, sometimes uncomfortable, and very real.

There was a little blood afterward and I was sore. I stared at it on the tissue and had the stupidest thought: so that's that. Danny asked three separate times if I was okay.

I finally told him if he asked again I was going to hit him with a pillow. I do not feel ruined.

I do not feel transformed into a different species. I feel close to him, shaky, guilty, happy, scared, and tired. I believe God exists.

I also believe I made this choice myself. Tomorrow morning I am supposed to go to church.

That will be interesting.

ENTRY 0404 — 2010-05-02 — Church the Morning After

I went to church. I almost did not.

Mom was making coffee and I considered claiming a headache. Then I got angry at the idea that I should hide from God like God somehow missed last night and would only learn about it if I sat in a pew.

So I went. Communion was hard. Not because lightning struck.

Because I kept thinking about Danny's hands and the soreness between my legs and the fact that the same body sitting in church was the body from last night. Mr.

Cline talked about integrity. He did not mention sex once. I cried quietly during the last prayer.

I am not sorry I was with Danny. I am not entirely free of guilt either.

Those things are both true and I refuse to lie about either one just to make the story cleaner. After church Mom asked whether prom was good. I said, “Yeah.

Really good.”

She looked at me for one second longer than normal. I have no idea what she knows.

ENTRY 0410 — 2010-05-28 — Graduation

I graduated. For real this time. No expulsion.

No repeated year. No asterisk. When they called “Tammy Lynn Mercer,” I heard my family yelling from somewhere behind the polite applause.

Mike was definitely the loudest. Dad stood up.

Mom cried. Shannon and I found each other afterward and screamed like idiots. I kept thinking about third grade.

Ten years ago I thought one terrible afternoon had decided what kind of person I was. Today I walked across a stage with good grades, a job, college acceptance paperwork, people who love me, and plans I am terrified of.

Danny came. He drove all that way and stood beside my family for pictures. For a few hours we looked exactly like the future I used to imagine.

I know better now than to call a photograph a promise. I still kept three copies.

ENTRY 0426 — 2010-08-23 — First Day at SWIC

First day at Southwestern Illinois College. I parked too far away because I was afraid of being late and then arrived twenty-five minutes early.

Very on brand. The campus is not glamorous. I love it anyway.

Nobody cares that I repeated third grade. Nobody knows about “Jugs.” Nobody knows I used to be the fat girl or the cheerleader or Danny Cole's girlfriend.

I am just Tammy Mercer with a notebook and a student ID that makes me look vaguely criminal. My English instructor assigned more reading than some people apparently expected. I wanted to hug her.

Then I worked the diner until close. That part is going to be the problem.

School from morning into afternoon. Work into night. Drive home.

Read. Sleep.

Repeat. I can do hard things. The question is whether hard is the same thing as sustainable.

I hated her for saying exactly what I needed.

I feel ashamed anyway. Education is supposed to be the ladder.

ENTRY 0493 — 2012-11-17 — The End Zone

Danny proposed to Amanda on television tonight. I watched it happen. I need to write the ugly part first.

I hated her. For maybe thirty seconds I hated a woman I have never met because she was standing inside the life I once thought belonged to me.

Then I hated myself because Amanda did not steal anything. She did not break into my house and take my future. She met a man at college.

He loved her. That is allowed.

The stadium was roaring. Everybody was cheering. Danny dropped to one knee in the end zone and I knew the look on his face.

Serious Danny. The face he makes when the joke is over and he means what comes next.

I felt physically sick. I thought about prom. I thought about the first time we had sex.

I thought about his letters. I thought about every game I watched like loyalty could build a bridge all the way to Texas.

Does she know about me? Probably. Maybe as “my high-school girlfriend.” Four words for something that once occupied my whole body.

I wanted to call him and scream, You said you loved me. He did love me.

That is the fucking problem. Love happened, and it still did not guarantee permanence. I turned the television off after they showed the replay for the third time.

I do not regret loving him. I regret believing love was a warranty.

ENTRY 0508 — 2013-03-14 — Diner University

Lisa caught me reading a psychology book behind the counter during the dead hour. She said, “You know normal waitresses do crossword puzzles.”

I said normal is an oppressive social construction. She threw a straw wrapper at me.

A trucker named Dennis came in later and told me his daughter will not speak to him. He wanted me to tell him she was ungrateful.

I would not. I asked what happened before she stopped answering. He stared at his coffee for a long time.

That is the thing I keep noticing: people usually tell you what they want the verdict to be before they tell you the evidence. I miss school.

I am also learning constantly here. The diner has terrible fluorescent lighting and no accreditation, but people bring their entire nervous systems through the door.

ENTRY 0542 — 2014-08-03 — Grandpa Walter

Grandpa Walter died today. Dad called me from the hospital before sunrise. I knew from his voice before he said the words. Grandpa looked stern in almost every photograph ever taken of him, but he was the man who let me hold real tools before other adults thought I should.

He never praised in paragraphs. If he said “good,” you remembered it for a week.

Dad is taking it harder than he admits. He spent half the afternoon fixing a loose hinge at Grandma June's house because apparently Mercers process grief with screwdrivers. I sat on the floor beside him and handed him tools.

Neither of us said much. I understand that language.
ENTRY 0543 — 2014-08-10 — The Books Keep Coming

I keep circling the same idea: understanding why somebody behaves badly does not mean pretending the harm disappears. Circumstances explain. Responsibility still exists.

Dad taught me that in third grade before I had vocabulary for it. I love being an aunt.

I also notice how quickly people assume loving children means I should explain why I do not have my own. Sometimes there is no answer that fits into polite conversation. I keep seeing the little grease crescent under my thumbnail, which is probably why I am writing instead of sleeping.

Lisa says I interrogate people. I say I ask follow-up questions.

It refuses. Probably healthy.

ENTRY 0558 — 2015-04-12 — The Cruise Folder

The cruise is real enough now that there is a folder on my dresser. Seven nights. Caribbean.

A cabin I do not have to clean for somebody else. Food I do not have to carry to a table.

Ocean I am not seeing through a diner window poster. I have saved for this for years in humiliating little increments. Twenty dollars.

Forty dollars. A tax refund.

Christmas money. The overtime week I thought I might die. Mom keeps saying I deserve it.

I keep saying “I know” like I believe people are allowed to deserve things without first proving nobody else needs the money more. I bought a stupid sun hat today.

It is enormous. I love it.
ENTRY 0559 — 2015-04-19 — Money

I bought myself food instead of eating something sad from home and then felt guilty about twelve dollars. I hate that poverty trains you to put every small pleasure on trial while expensive people call their pleasures lifestyle.

Dad hates being treated like a patient.

ENTRY 0566 — 2015-06-09 — Dad's Heart

Dad had a heart attack. Those words still do not fit in the same sentence as him.

Ray Mercer is supposed to be the person who carries the heavy thing, figures out why the engine will not turn over, drives through sleet because somebody needs picking up. He is not supposed to be a pale man in a hospital bed with adhesive pads on his chest. Mom called me from the ambulance bay.

I do not remember driving there. Jason got there first.

Mike came straight from a job in his work clothes. Dad made a joke about everybody finally getting together without baseball. Then he closed his eyes because even talking tired him out.

The cruise folder is still on my dresser. I looked at it when I got home to shower.

I started crying because I might lose my father. Then, in the middle of that, I cried because I knew the cruise was gone too. I felt like a monster for caring about both.

I am writing this so I cannot lie later: I was terrified Dad would die, and I was heartbroken about my trip. Two griefs can exist in one body.

ENTRY 0569 — 2015-06-14 — Cancelled

I cancelled the cruise today. The woman on the phone was kind, which almost made it worse.

Some of the money comes back. Some does not.

I stopped caring about the exact percentages after the third call. Dad needs prescriptions. Mom needs gas money and groceries because hospital parking seems designed by people who hate families.

There is a gap before short-term disability does whatever it is supposed to do. I transferred money from the cruise account.

I sat there looking at the balance afterward. Years of tiny deposits vanished in under two minutes. Dad did not ask me to do it.

That matters. I did it because I love him and because the bills are real.

I am still sad about the fucking sun hat.
