ENTRY 0585 — 2015-09-22 — No More Welding

The doctor told Dad he cannot go back to welding. Not “take a few more weeks.” Not “light duty.” Cannot. The testing finally put names on what has been happening inside him: lungs damaged by years of fumes and particulates, cardiovascular damage layered over it, a body that paid interest on every shift he ever worked around smoke and metal.

Dad sat completely still. I watched his face when the doctor said the word permanently. He asked practical questions.

Disability. Restrictions.

Oxygen numbers. Medication. He did not ask who he is supposed to be if he is not a welder.

That question was in the room anyway. On the drive home he stared out the window and said, “Forty years and they tell you to stop in one sentence.”

I did not know what to say.

So I drove.

ENTRY 0641 — 2017-05-08 — Mechanic Enough

A man at the parts store explained my own alternator problem back to me today. I told him I had already checked the belt, battery voltage, grounds, and charging output and that the alternator was dropping out hot.

He still started with “Well, sweetheart...”

I nearly levitated. Dad laughed so hard when I told him that he started coughing. Then he said, “Did you buy the right alternator?”

I said yes.

He said, “Then who cares what he thought?”

That is Dad's superpower. He can make dignity sound like torque specification.

I installed it myself. The charging voltage came up exactly where it should. I stood there with greasy hands and felt absurdly happy.

ENTRY 0673 — 2018-10-06 — Not the Little Doll

Something embarrassing happened in my head today. A group of younger men came into the diner after a game. Ten years ago I would have been bracing myself for stares, jokes, somebody trying too hard to flirt.

They barely noticed me. My first feeling was relief.

My second feeling hurt. I am ashamed of the second one. I spent years wanting men to stop looking at my breasts before my face.

Now some part of me apparently wants a certificate proving I am still worth looking at. That is infuriating.

I am twenty-seven, almost twenty-eight. I am not ancient. My body is fuller than it was at nineteen.

My face is changing in tiny ways only I probably see. I do not want to become a woman who measures her existence by random male attention.

But I also cannot heal something by pretending it is not there. Being desired is not the same as being known. Maybe I have used one as counterfeit proof of the other more than I understood.

ENTRY 0688 — 2019-06-12 — Mrs. Keene and the Social Worker

Mrs. Keene came in with a woman named Angela from a community agency. I expected the kind of professional voice that makes poor people feel like forms with shoes.

Angela was different. Keene had a housing notice, two medical bills, and a benefits letter she could not make sense of.

Angela put everything in piles, explained one thing at a time, and never once made Mrs. Keene feel stupid. When Mrs.

Keene got embarrassed about not understanding a paragraph, Angela said, “This is badly written. That's not the same as you being bad at reading.”

I nearly stopped breathing.

That sentence. That is what I want people to feel when they talk to me. Not rescued.

Not managed. Less alone and less ashamed.

I asked Angela later what her job title is. Social worker. I came home and looked it up.

ENTRY 0742 — 2021-11-06 — Thirty-One

Thirty-one. I did arithmetic tonight and made myself miserable. If I go back to school next year, then transfer, then graduate, then maybe graduate school, I will be how old? If I meet somebody at thirty-two, date for two years, marry, try for children—stop.

Stop. I turned my life into a train schedule and then panicked because I could not see all the connections.

I want children. There. That is the thing underneath the math.

I love Tyler and Emily and Sophie and Luke so much it hurts. I can hold one of them and feel pure joy, then drive home alone and cry.

That does not make their existence a wound. It means my joy and grief occupy neighboring rooms. I will not marry somebody merely because my ovaries have begun issuing deadlines.

I also will not pretend time is imaginary. Thirty-one feels young until you want something biology has opinions about.

ENTRY 0751 — 2022-01-08 — EXIT

I opened a separate savings account today. I named it EXIT.

The banker did not see the nickname. Good. I put $300 in it.

Three hundred dollars is not a new life. It is barely a set of tires.

ENTRY 0783 — 2023-09-09 — Dad Told Me to Go

Dad told me to leave. Not tonight. Not angrily.

We were in the driveway with the Barracuda hood up. He gets tired faster now and had to sit on the folding stool while I traced a vacuum leak.

Out of nowhere he said, “You know we don't get to keep needing you forever.”

I thought I misheard him. He said I have already given this family years. He said Mom will manage.

Jason and Mike will step up if they have to. He said being the available one became a habit and habits start pretending they are duties.

I cried. Dad looked uncomfortable, because of course he did. Then he said, “Jesus, Tammy, I'm not dying today.

Stop looking at me like that.”

I laughed while crying. He told me to finish school somewhere.

Go somewhere I picked. Make my life inconvenient for somebody besides myself. I think he knows about EXIT.

Maybe Mom told him. Maybe fathers know things.

ENTRY 0809 — 2024-04-20 — The Title

Dad signed the Barracuda over to me today. I told him not to.

He told me not to be stupid. The title sat on the kitchen table between his pill organizer and Mom's grocery list.

He signed carefully because his hand shakes more now. He said, “It's yours whether you stay or go.”

I said it is his car. He said, “It's a machine.

I'm your father. Learn the difference.”

That broke me.

The Heart tape is still in the 8-track. I drove around the block afterward and cried so hard I had to wipe my face at stop signs. The car feels heavier now even though nothing mechanical changed.

ENTRY 0817 — 2024-06-19 — Dad

ENTRY 0818 — 2024-06-27 — Charleston

ENTRY 0820 — 2024-07-11 — Charleston

ENTRY 0821 — 2024-07-19 — Dad

Dad died today. I cannot make the sentence bigger than that.

He died today. Mom and I were at the hospital. Jason and Mike got there before the end, but time is broken and I cannot put the order together properly.

There were machines. Nurses.

Somebody asking whether we understood. Dad's hand felt warm until it didn't. I kept looking at his fingernails.

Those hands built things. Burned things.

Fixed my bike. Showed me how not to cross-thread a bolt. Held a welding torch for decades.

Pointed at the Barracuda engine while he made me explain what I thought was wrong before he would tell me. Then there was just his hand inside mine and nothing left for either of us to fix.

Mom made a sound I have never heard from her before. I do not know what happens next. I do not know how the world gets to have tomorrow in it.
ENTRY 0822 — 2024-07-21 — The Phone Calls

Two days since Dad died.

The house keeps filling with phone calls. People who loved him. People who worked with him twenty years ago. People Mom barely remembers. Every call begins with the same careful voice, and every time I hear it I brace myself.

Jason is handling some of the family calls. Mike is dealing with the funeral home. I somehow became the person with the folder: insurance, hospital papers, Dad's ID, medication list, the notes from the doctor, names and numbers written on envelopes because nobody had a clean piece of paper when somebody called.

Mom keeps asking where something is and then apologizing for asking. I told her to stop apologizing. This is exactly what I am good at.

That sentence made me cry in the bathroom.

Being good at the paperwork does not mean I am good at Dad being dead.

ENTRY 0823 — 2024-07-22 — His Things

Dad's reading glasses are still on the table beside the insurance papers.

Nobody has moved them.

His work boots are near the door. There is a shop rag in one back pocket of the jeans Mom washed before the hospital. His coffee cup is in the cabinet where it always was. Nothing understands that it has changed categories. Yesterday these were Dad's things. Now everybody keeps saying "his things" in the tone people use for belongings left behind.

I went into the garage and immediately walked back out.

The Barracuda was sitting there with the hood down. His socket set was exactly where he left it. I could smell oil, old vinyl, metal, and that faint ghost of welding that seems permanently soaked into the garage.

I know he signed the car over to me months ago.

It still feels like his car.

Maybe it always will.

ENTRY 0824 — 2024-07-24 — Walnut Hill

We buried Dad at Walnut Hill Cemetery today. 1101 Mascoutah Avenue, Belleville. Section 15, Lot 248, Grave 3.

I memorized it because apparently my brain needs grief to have coordinates.

The grass was too green. The sky was offensively beautiful. Somebody's phone rang during the service and I wanted to kill them, then felt guilty because Dad would have thought that was funny.

Mom held my hand on one side. Jason on the other. Mike stood close enough that our shoulders touched.

After people left, I stayed.

I told Dad I still have the Barracuda. I told him I am going to take care of Mom. Then I stopped because I could almost hear him telling me not to turn taking care of everybody into an excuse for never having a life of my own.

So I told him something else.

I told him I am still going to go.

I do not know whether I was promising him or myself.

ENTRY 0825 — 2024-07-25 — After the Burial

The day after burying somebody should not contain errands.

It does.

There was a death certificate question, a pharmacy call, a bank form, food somebody brought over in a foil pan, three sympathy cards, and a stupid problem with the printer. I wanted the entire world to understand that Dad was buried yesterday and therefore printers should work without complaint for at least a week.

Mom ate half a piece of toast and claimed she was not hungry. I made her eat soup later.

I went back to Walnut Hill near sunset. I sat beside the grave and talked to Dad more than I usually talked when he was alive. That is probably because he cannot interrupt me with practical advice now.

I told him I was angry.

I did not explain what at.

Everything, probably.

ENTRY 0826 — 2024-07-27 — Mom Knows

Mom knows I am still thinking about leaving Illinois.

I had not planned to talk about it this week. It felt obscene somehow, like discussing my future too close to Dad's funeral meant I was already walking away from him.

Mom brought it up.

She said, "Your father didn't give you that car so you could park it here forever."

I told her I was not leaving her right now.

She said that was not what she said.

Then we had one of our quiet fights where neither of us raises a voice and both of us become extremely interested in wiping already-clean counters.

Finally she said, "Tammy, I lost my husband. I don't need to lose my daughter because she decides grief is a job."

That shut me up.

I still do not know when I am going.

But Mom knows I intend to.

ENTRY 0827 — 2024-07-28 — The Quiet After

The house was full of people for days and now suddenly it is not.

That might be worse.

There are leftovers in the refrigerator from women at church. A stack of cards on the table. Flowers beginning to droop. Mom is sitting in Dad's chair even though she never used to sit there.

I went to the diner for a few hours because Lisa told me I could come in, leave, wash dishes, scream in the freezer, or do absolutely nothing and she would count all of those as reasonable behavior.

I poured coffee. I carried plates. Earl complained about the Cardinals like the universe had not ended.

It helped.

Not because I forgot Dad.

Because for four hours nobody needed me to understand a form, answer a condolence, or decide what happens to a dead man's account.

Sometimes ordinary work is mercy.

ENTRY 0828 — 2024-07-30 — Paperwork

Death has an administrative department.

Nobody mentions this at funerals.

Copies of the death certificate. Insurance. Hospital billing. Social Security. Bank accounts. Vehicle records even though the Barracuda title is already mine. Mom's beneficiary paperwork. A welding pension question. Every office wants a number from another office.

I made a checklist.

Of course I did.

Jason offered to take half of it. I gave him the pension calls because he has a lower tolerance for hold music but a higher tolerance for arguing with institutions. Mike took the bank.

I kept the medical and insurance paperwork because I already know the history.

For years being the available daughter meant I knew everything.

For once that knowledge is useful to all of us.

I hate the reason.

ENTRY 0829 — 2024-07-31 — Grief Sneaks Up

I made it through almost the whole day without crying.

Then I found one of Dad's pencils in the Barracuda glove box.

Not a special pencil. Short, flat carpenter pencil, sharpened with a knife instead of a pencil sharpener because apparently normal pencils were too civilized for him.

I sat in the driver's seat holding it and lost my mind.

This is what grief does. The funeral did not break me the way the pencil did.

Later Shannon called and let me talk for almost an hour. I told her I feel guilty whenever I laugh. She said that sounds exactly like something my brain would invent to make grief harder than it already is.

She is probably right.

Dad liked making me laugh.

I am trying to remember that without turning it into another rule.

ENTRY 0830 — 2024-08-02 — Insurance

I spent forty-three minutes on hold today.

I know because I wrote the time down.

The woman who finally answered was kind. That almost made it worse. She needed dates and policy numbers and the exact date of death, and every time I said July 19 out loud it felt newly impossible.

Mom sat across from me with both hands around coffee she was not drinking.

When we finished she asked whether there was anything else.

I said no.

There is always something else.

But there was nothing else I was willing to do today.

I took the Barracuda out and drove with Heart playing through the old 8-track. The tape changed tracks with that clunk I have heard since childhood.

I cried at a red light.

Then it turned green, because traffic remains offensively committed to continuing.

ENTRY 0831 — 2024-08-03 — The Empty Chair

Grandpa Walter died ten years ago today.

I realized that while looking at Dad's empty chair.

Apparently August has decided to collect men in my family.

Mom said that was a terrible thing to say and then laughed anyway, which made both of us cry.

We went to Walnut Hill in the afternoon. Dad's grave still looks temporary. Fresh dirt, flowers, the rawness of a place that has not had time to become part of the landscape.

Mom stood there silently.

I talked enough for both of us.

On the way home she asked me to drive. She usually prefers to drive her own car.

I understood what she was asking without her saying it.

I drove.

ENTRY 0832 — 2024-08-05 — Mom's Anger

Mom is angry today.

Not at me exactly. At insurance, the hospital, Dad, God, the broken latch on the kitchen cabinet, and probably every person who has ever said "he's in a better place" without being invited to.

She snapped at me for moving an envelope.

I snapped back.

Then we stood in the kitchen staring at each other because both of us knew the envelope was not the problem.

She said, "I don't know how to be a widow."

I said, "I don't know how to be the daughter of a dead father."

That sounds melodramatic written down.

It did not feel melodramatic in the kitchen.

It felt like two women admitting we have no idea what we are doing.

ENTRY 0833 — 2024-08-06 — Grief Sneaks Up Again

A man at the diner had Dad's hands.

Not his face. Not his voice. Just thick fingers, scarred knuckles, dark half-moons of grease near the nails.

I was pouring his coffee and suddenly could not breathe correctly.

Lisa saw my face, took the pot from me, and told me to go outside.

I sat behind the diner near the delivery door and cried beside a stack of empty milk crates.

The man never knew.

That is strange to me. He went home after lunch having no idea his hands detonated somebody else's grief.

I came back in ten minutes later.

Lisa did not ask whether I was okay.

She asked whether I wanted table four or table six.

I love her for knowing the difference.

ENTRY 0834 — 2024-08-08 — Charleston

I looked at Charleston apartments online again tonight.

Then I looked at tuition.

Then jobs.

Then I closed every tab because the numbers made the whole idea feel stupid.

An hour later I opened them again.

Dad knew I wanted out. Not out of the family. Out of the shape my life had taken because I was always the available one.

I keep hearing something he said before he got really sick: "You can love people from another zip code."

At the time I accused him of trying to get rid of me.

He said, "No. I'm trying to get you to quit hiding behind us."

That was Dad. Tenderness delivered with all the cushioning of a socket wrench.

Charleston still scares me.

The fact that it scares me is becoming less persuasive.

ENTRY 0835 — 2024-08-09 — The Barracuda

I cleaned the Barracuda today.

Actually cleaned it. Vacuumed under the seats, wiped the dash, sorted the glove box, checked fluids, tightened the battery connection, and found three things Dad had left in it that I could not throw away.

The carpenter pencil from last week.

A faded gas receipt.

A tiny folded paper with a part number written in his handwriting.

None of those things are valuable.

All of them are now apparently sacred relics because grief is ridiculous.

The Heart 8-track is still in the deck.

I played it while I worked.

For the first time since Dad died, the car felt a little less like a coffin-shaped memory and a little more like the machine he meant me to have.

ENTRY 0836 — 2024-08-11 — Dad's Tools

Jason, Mike, and I went through Dad's tools.

That sentence sounds simple.

It was not.

Every drawer had a history. The good torque wrench. The cheap sockets Dad kept for loaning to people he did not trust. Welding clamps. Electrical testers. Files. Hammers polished smooth where his hands held them.

Jason wanted the big rolling chest because he has the garage space. Mike wanted Dad's old welding hood even though none of us thinks he should actually use it.

I wanted the multimeter and the small socket set Dad taught me on.

Nobody fought.

That made it harder somehow.

We kept asking, "You want this?" in voices that were too careful.

Dad would have hated how solemn we were being about wrenches.

ENTRY 0837 — 2024-08-12 — Three Weeks

Three weeks and a few days.

I keep expecting grief to become more organized.

It does not.

Mom is sleeping badly. I know because her light is on when I get up for water. I am sleeping badly too, but we have both apparently agreed not to discuss this because then somebody might suggest doing something sensible.

Church people still bring food occasionally. The frequency is dropping, which is natural and also feels like a second abandonment even though that is unfair.

I went to work, came home, checked on Mom, and read twenty pages of a book about trauma before realizing I had absorbed none of it.

So I started again.

That is basically my strategy for everything right now.

Start again.

ENTRY 0838 — 2024-08-13 — Mom and Me

Mom and I ate Chinese takeout at the kitchen table tonight.

No television.

No paperwork.

No one visiting.

We talked about Dad before he was sick.

She told me about a date they had in 1983 when his truck broke down and he tried to fix it in dress shoes because he had been attempting to impress her. I have heard pieces of the story before, but not the part where Mom sat on the hood laughing while he swore at the starter.

I asked whether she knew she would marry him.

She said, "Not that night. That night I thought he was an idiot."

I laughed so hard I nearly choked on rice.

It felt good.

Then it hurt.

Then it felt good again.

Apparently those feelings can share a table.

ENTRY 0839 — 2024-08-15 — Charleston Again

I priced the move again.

Gas. Deposit. First month. School. Emergency money. Car repairs before I trust the Barracuda that far. Job gap if I do not have work lined up.

I made a spreadsheet.

Of course I made a spreadsheet.

The number at the bottom is not impossible.

That is new.

For years Charleston was a fantasy I could protect by never calculating it. Now it has numbers attached.

Numbers are dangerous because they can turn wanting into planning.

I am not leaving tomorrow.

Mom needs time. I need time. There are still things to settle here.

But I am starting to think in months instead of someday.

ENTRY 0840 — 2024-08-16 — Mom Knows How Serious I Am

Mom found the Charleston spreadsheet.

I left it on the printer.

Smooth.

She read the first page before I came back into the room and then gave me the look she used when I was sixteen and trying to explain why the Barracuda mysteriously had less gas than it should.

I said, "I'm not leaving you next week."

She said, "Tammy, stop making me the excuse."

That hurt because it was accurate.

We talked for almost two hours.

She is afraid of the trailer being quiet. I am afraid of leaving and discovering I am less capable than I think. We both admitted those things.

Then Mom said she would rather miss me in South Carolina than watch me resent everybody here ten years from now.

I wrote that down immediately after she went to bed.

ENTRY 0841 — 2024-08-18 — His Work Shirts

Mom asked me to help sort Dad's clothes.

I lasted twelve minutes.

His work shirts were the worst. The old heavy ones with tiny burns, frayed cuffs, and the faint smell that no amount of laundry ever completely removed. Welding fumes, metal dust, sweat, machine shop, Dad.

Mom picked up one blue shirt and pressed it against her face.

I left the room because seeing that felt too private even though she is my mother and he was my father.

We kept some.

Donated some.

Threw away almost nothing today.

There is no law saying grief has to be efficient.

ENTRY 0842 — 2024-08-19 — Brothers

Jason, Mike, and I had dinner without Mom tonight.

That almost never happens.

We talked about Dad. Then baseball. Then Dad again. Then money. Then whether Mom should stay in the trailer long term.

Jason thinks she eventually needs something easier to maintain. Mike thinks bringing it up now would be cruel. I think both things can be true.

At one point Jason apologized for how much baseball swallowed the family when we were kids.

I stared at him.

He said, "I know you noticed."

I told him of course I noticed.

Then I told him I never blamed him for being good at something people celebrated.

That is mostly true now.

It was less true when I was thirteen.

We are getting old enough to forgive each other for childhood things nobody actually designed.

ENTRY 0843 — 2024-08-21 — The Hospital Bill

A hospital bill came today.

There is something grotesque about a bill arriving after the patient is dead.

I know that is how systems work. Services happened. Codes exist. Insurance has to process them. Understanding the mechanism does not make the envelope less ugly.

I called because one line item did not match the explanation of benefits.

The person on the phone corrected it.

A small victory.

Dad would have been proud that I checked instead of paying whatever number they printed.

That thought made me smile.

Then I cried because I cannot call and tell him I caught it.

This is grief too, apparently: having information with nowhere to send it.

ENTRY 0844 — 2024-08-22 — The Garage

I worked in the garage for almost three hours today.

Nothing dramatic. Battery maintenance on the Barracuda, cleaning connections, checking belts and hoses, putting tools back where they actually belong instead of where grief abandoned them.

I caught myself saying, "Okay, Dad, what am I missing?" out loud.

Then I answered myself in his voice: start simple.

Battery. Cable. Ground. Fuel. Spark. Do not replace what you have not tested.

I know those rules because he repeated them until they became part of the way I think.

That is a strange kind of inheritance.

Not the car.

The diagnostic sequence in my head.

ENTRY 0845 — 2024-08-24 — Mike's Birthday

Mike's birthday.

Thirty-seven.

First birthday in the immediate family without Dad.

Nobody said that sentence at dinner, but it sat at the table anyway.

Mom made cake. Jason brought beer. The kids were loud enough to rescue us from becoming solemn.

Mike opened a small box from Mom and froze.

Dad had bought him a ratcheting wrench set months ago and apparently told Mom it was for his birthday.

For one second Mike looked ten years old.

He went outside after that.

I followed eventually but did not talk much.

We stood beside the Barracuda and drank beer in the driveway.

Sometimes being somebody's sister means knowing silence is the useful thing.

ENTRY 0846 — 2024-08-25 — Sunday

Church was difficult today.

Not because anybody did anything wrong.

People are kind. That is almost the problem. Every hug asks me to feel Dad's absence again in public.

Mr. Cline talked about grief without pretending faith makes it painless. I appreciated that more than I expected.

Afterward an older woman told Mom, "At least you had forty years."

Mom smiled politely.

In the car she said, "Apparently forty years means I shouldn't want forty-one."

I laughed and then immediately hated myself for laughing.

Mom said, "Oh stop. It was funny."

So I stopped apologizing.

ENTRY 0847 — 2024-08-27 — Grief Sneaks Up

I was fine until I heard an air compressor kick on at a shop down the road.

That sound belongs to Dad in my head.

I pulled into a parking lot and sat there with both hands on the steering wheel.

The Heart tape was in the deck but I did not turn it on.

Sometimes music helps.

Sometimes it reaches too far inside.

I waited five minutes, wiped my face, and went to work.

Lisa took one look at me and handed me coffee without asking.

I am becoming very aware of the people who know how to help without making help into a performance.

I want to be that kind of person.

ENTRY 0848 — 2024-08-28 — Charleston Has a Date Range

Charleston has moved from "someday" to a date range.

Next summer.

Writing that makes my stomach drop.

Summer 2025 gives Mom almost a year. It gives me time to build savings, make sure the Barracuda is trustworthy, research school, line up work, and avoid turning grief into a runaway decision.

I went to Walnut Hill and told Dad.

I said, "Next summer. Probably."

Then I laughed because he would have hated the word probably.

He would have asked for a target date.

So I said June.

There. Happy now?

A bird landed on the grass nearby, which is not a sign from Dad because Dad would be deeply offended by being represented as a small brown bird.

ENTRY 0849 — 2024-08-30 — Mom Knows the Date Range

I told Mom I am thinking June 2025.

She went very still.

Then she nodded.

No drama. No accusation. That almost made it harder.

We talked about practical things because that is what Mercers do when emotion gets too large. What needs to happen with the trailer. Whether she wants Jason or Mike checking in on certain days. What I can take. What I should leave. Whether the Barracuda needs new tires before a long interstate drive.

Then Mom said, "You are allowed to be excited, you know."

I told her I am not excited.

She said, "Liar."

I am scared.

I am grieving.

I am also excited.

There. I wrote it.

ENTRY 0850 — 2024-08-31 — One Month Forward

Tomorrow is September.

Dad has been dead six weeks tomorrow, give or take the way my brain counts things now.

The future has started moving again against my wishes.

I spent part of today making a Charleston folder. School information. Job notes. Cost estimates. South Carolina residency rules. A list of things the Barracuda needs before I trust it for a move.

Then I put the folder away and went with Mom to Walnut Hill.

We sat near Dad without talking much.

On the drive home Mom said, "He'd be proud of you."

I said, "He'd tell me my budget is too optimistic."

She laughed.

That felt like a blessing I actually believe in.

ENTRY 0851 — 2024-09-05 — The Barracuda

I noticed the little grease crescent under my thumbnail, and it stuck with me. The Barracuda smelled like old vinyl, gasoline, and summer heat.

Ann Wilson's voice filling the car louder than the engine fan Dad pretended not to sing along. I caught him tapping two fingers on the steering wheel and chose not to ruin it by pointing it out. I keep seeing a library due-date slip sticking out of my book.

the wind kept lifting the hair off my neck. I worked, ate a Burger King Whopper and fries that tasted better because I was exhausted, answered messages, and thought too much while driving.

Every practical step toward Charleston makes the move less romantic and more possible. What I remember most clearly is Dad’s reading glasses on top of the insurance forms. What I remember most clearly is a diner ticket with somebody’s phone number on the back.
ENTRY 0852 — 2024-09-30 — Grief Sneaks Up

That is the part of September 30, 2024 I do not want to sand smooth later. Grief does not arrive only when I am thinking about Dad. It ambushes me through smells, tools, a phrase, a man with the same shoulders in a parking lot. Then it is gone and I am buying toothpaste like nothing happened.

I keep seeing a medication bottle beside Mom’s grocery list, which is probably why I am writing instead of sleeping. Jason called about something practical and somehow we ended up talking for forty minutes.

He is still capable of sounding like the older brother who got all the applause, but adulthood has made him softer around the edges. I do not think he knows how much I notice. I keep seeing a diner ticket with somebody’s phone number on the back, which is probably why I am writing instead of sleeping.

Mom is learning to let me leave without calling it rejection.

I hate that poverty trains you to put every small pleasure on trial while expensive people call their pleasures lifestyle.

ENTRY 0861 — 2025-05-09 — I Gave Notice

I gave Lisa my notice today. She stared at the paper, then at me.

“Charleston?”

“Yes.”

“South Carolina?”

“I am aware there are other Charlestons.”

She came around the counter and hugged me so hard I dropped my pen. Then she called me an asshole for making her cry during lunch rush. I have worked at this diner so long I know which floor tile rocks near table six.

I know the freezer door has to be lifted before it closes. I know Dennis takes two sugars and Mrs.

Keene pretends she wants decaf but will accept regular if you do not make a big thing of it. Leaving feels like tearing fabric. It also feels like breathing.

ENTRY 0899 — 2025-06-30 — Eastbound

I left Illinois today. Mom stood beside the Barracuda crying and pretending she was not crying.

Jason checked the tires even though I checked them yesterday. Mike put a small socket set in the trunk like I do not already own tools.

Grandma June gave me sandwiches wrapped in foil she has probably been saving since the Clinton administration. I drove to Walnut Hill first. I sat by Dad's grave and told him, “I'm going.”

Then I got in his car—my car—and pointed east.

The Heart 8-track clunked into place somewhere after I crossed into Indiana. I laughed out loud when “Barracuda” came on because of course it did.

I am thirty-four years old. I have tools, books, clothes, an EXIT account that is about to become much smaller, and no guarantee that any of this works. For once that does not feel like evidence I should stay.

It feels like the price of leaving.

ENTRY 0901 — 2025-07-02 — Charleston

I am in Charleston. The air is thick enough to drink.

Everything smells green, salty, hot, or expensive. I got lost twice. The Barracuda attracted three comments before lunch, including one man who asked whether it was “really mine.” I said no, I stole it from a museum.

I have temporary housing and a list of things to do that is longer than my arm: driver's license, registration, school paperwork, work, bank address, groceries, figure out where I am actually going to live. I drove out toward Johns Island tonight just to see water.

I parked and sat with the windows down. For the first time in my life, nobody in driving distance knows what I am supposed to be doing tomorrow. That is terrifying.

It is also the closest thing to freedom I have ever felt.

ENTRY 0908 — 2025-07-21 — Hyatt Front Desk

First real week at the Hyatt is done. Front desk is basically diner work with nicer clothes, computers, luggage, room keys, and people who have paid enough money to believe inconvenience should not exist. I am good at it.

That surprised me less than it probably should. People tell you what they need with posture before words.

The furious man whose reservation was wrong was mostly afraid he had ruined his wife's anniversary. The woman snapping about parking was exhausted and traveling alone with two children. The drunk convention guy actually needed a boundary, not sympathy.

Marisol calls me Professor because I used the phrase “behavioral pattern” about a guest. She is going to be trouble.

Good trouble, I think.

ENTRY 0929 — 2025-10-03 — I Bought the Boat

I bought the Chris-Craft today. Writing that sentence makes me sound much richer than I am.

1980 Chris-Craft 35. Old teak.

Worn interior. One engine that runs and one engine that is currently an expensive piece of iron occupying space. Price: $25,000.

I put $5,000 down. Frank is carrying the other $20,000 himself at zero interest.

Fifty payments of $400. First one November 1. No balloon.

No prepayment penalty. I read the agreement three times.

Frank said he would rather have predictable money from somebody who understands old machinery than spend another year paying around a boat he does not want to repair. I said, “You understand I am buying the problem, right?”

He said, “You're buying the boat. The problem comes free.”

I laughed for five straight minutes.

I stood inside afterward with the keys in my hand. This thing is older than I am and needs work.

Apparently I have a type.

ENTRY 0934 — 2025-11-01 — First Four Hundred

First boat payment: $400. I sent it to Frank this morning and then stared at my account balance like the number might apologize.

There is something very adult about owing money on a boat with a dead engine while also having homework due Sunday night. I ate Chinese takeout on board and made a spreadsheet of bills.

Boat note. Slip note. Dues.

Electric. Insurance.

Phone. Hotspot. Gas for a 318 V8 designed in an era when fuel was apparently a rumor.

I am not drowning. But I can see the waterline.

That is probably useful on a boat.

ENTRY 0966 — 2026-05-12 — 3.82

Grades posted. 3.82 GPA for my first full year back.

I stared at the number on my phone until Marisol asked whether somebody died. I told her. She screamed loud enough that a guest turned around.

I know grades are not intelligence. I learned that lesson the hard way in both directions.

But this number matters to me because it is evidence. I did the work while working nights. I did the work while living on an old boat.

I did the work while fixing pumps and driving an old V8 and missing Dad and sometimes eating dinner out of a paper bag at 11:45 p.m. I did not fail to become educated because I was incapable.

I got interrupted. There is a difference.

ENTRY 0974 — 2026-06-30 — One Year in South Carolina

One year ago today I drove out of Illinois. Today I sorted my South Carolina residency documents into a folder.

Driver's license. Registration.

Tax return. Lease/ownership paperwork. Pay records.

Bank statements. Evidence that I actually support myself.

I know one year in the state does not automatically make USC say yes to resident tuition. There is an application and they will decide whether I meet the rules. But I built the year.

Day by day. That feels bigger than paperwork.

I stood on the dock tonight and called Mom. She said Dad would be unbearable about how proud he was. I said, “Yeah.”

Then neither of us talked for a few seconds.

ENTRY 0986 — 2026-09-03 — Professor Patel

Professor Patel stopped me after class. She asked whether I had started talking to USC admissions and whether I understood how competitive transfer work can get once life schedules are involved.

Then she said, “You think like a psychologist, Tammy. Do not confuse being late to the credential with being late to the subject.”

I wrote that sentence down in the parking lot before I could ruin it with modesty. Statistics is still kicking my ass in very specific places.

But I am beginning to see what the equations are trying to protect us from: the human talent for seeing patterns we want to see. That seems relevant to basically everything.

ENTRY 0994 — 2026-10-03 — One Year Owning the Chris-Craft

One year since I bought the boat. Twelve $400 payments made.

$4,800 off the $20,000 balance. About $15,200 left.

The starboard engine starts reliably now if I respect the ritual. The port engine is still the long argument. I have replaced hoses, chased grounds, sworn at a freshwater pump, cleaned bilge spaces no human body was designed to enter, and learned that “marine grade” means the same object costs three times as much because it has been blessed by salt water.

Eli brought over a compression gauge and then, importantly, let me use it instead of taking over. I like him for that.

This boat is not fixed. Neither am I. I do not mean that sadly.

Working condition is not the same thing as finished.

ENTRY 1000 — 2026-10-20 — Tuesday Night, Home

I got home late from the Hyatt tonight. Home means the Chris-Craft now. I still sometimes catch myself thinking the word and being surprised.

I changed out of work clothes, heated leftover Chinese, and opened my statistics notes at the little table. The boat shifted against the lines when somebody passed in the channel. Somewhere outside, a halyard or piece of rigging tapped metal in the same rhythm for ten minutes.

My phone says I owe Frank $400 again on November 1. The slip payment will come too.

Electric, insurance, tuition, books, gas, groceries. Adulthood has not become poetic just because I moved near salt water. The Barracuda is in the lot with Dad's old Heart 8-track still in the deck.

I drove it to class this morning. The tape changed tracks with that mechanical clunk I have heard since I was a kid, and for a second Dad was so close I could almost feel him in the passenger seat telling me the idle sounded high.

I have one good year of college behind me and another underway. 3.82 GPA. USC psychology is not a fantasy anymore; it is paperwork, prerequisites, money, residency documentation, and work I can actually do.

After that, social work still feels right. I want to be the person who can sit across from somebody ashamed and make the system smaller instead of making them feel smaller.

I am thirty-five. I still want children. I still miss Dad.

I still sometimes think about Danny when football is on. I still tug at my shirt some days and hear old voices that should have died twenty years ago.

I still get angry too fast when somebody weaker is being cornered. None of that means I am stuck. Tomorrow I have class, then the Hyatt, then I need to trace the port-engine ignition problem again if I have enough light.

It is not a glamorous life. It is mine.
